# PART A — arXiv 2510.02410

## Identity

**Yes, this IS the OpenTSLM paper.**

- **Title:** *OpenTSLM: Time-Series Language Models for Reasoning over Multivariate Medical Text- and Time-Series Data*
- **Authors:** Patrick Langer (corresponding, planger@stanford.edu), Thomas Kaar, Max Rosenblattl, Maxwell A. Xu, Winnie Chow, Martin Maritsch, Robert Jakob, Ning Wang, Juncheng Liu, Aradhana Verma, Brian Han, Daniel Seung Kim, Henry Chubb, Scott Ceresnak, Aydin Zahedivash, Alexander Tarlochan Singh Sandhu, Fatima Rodriguez, Daniel McDuff, Elgar Fleisch, Oliver Aalami, Filipe Barata, Paul Schmiedmayer
- **Affiliations:** Stanford Mussallem Center for Biodesign (Stanford University); Agentic Systems Lab, ETH Zurich; Centre for Digital Health Interventions, ETH Zurich; UIUC; Google Research; Amazon
- **Dates:** v1 2 Oct 2025 · v2 12 Feb 2026 · v3 14 Feb 2026 (cs.LG, CC BY 4.0)
- **Code/models:** https://github.com/StanfordBDHG/OpenTSLM (mirror: github.com/OpenTSLM/OpenTSLM), `pip install opentslm`, MIT licence. HF models: `OpenTSLM/{llama-3.2-1b|llama-3.2-3b|gemma-3-1b-pt|gemma-3-270m}-{tsqa|m4|har|sleep|ecg}-{sp|flamingo}`

## Core claim / problem

LLMs handle text and images natively but not raw time series. Tokenizing a series as digits blows up context (12 ECG leads × 10 s ≈ **80k tokens, &gt;100 GB VRAM/sample**) and text-only LLMs collapse to ~0–9% F1. OpenTSLM makes time series a **native modality** of a pretrained LLM, supports **any number of series of any length**, and — critically — produces **free-form chain-of-thought rationales** ending in a parseable answer, rather than a classification head.

Headline numbers (from abstract): 69.88% F1 sleep staging, 65.44% HAR (encoder from scratch); 68.33% / 67.64% with a pretrained TSFM (Chronos-2); vs 9.05% / 60.44% for fine-tuned text-only baselines, and 2.95% (text) / 10.83% (plot) for GPT-4o on HAR.

---

## THE DATA RECIPE (the part you care about)

### The core move

&gt; "For each dataset, we generated rationales with **GPT-4o** by **providing a plot of the data along with the correct label**, and prompting the model to produce a rationale leading to that label. … We carefully engineered the prompts and manually reviewed a subset of samples to ensure the generated rationales were consistent and sensible. **When plotting, original data was used without normalization.** If multiple time-series were present in a sample (e.g., three in HAR or twelve in ECG), all were plotted as separate subplots but combined into a single figure."

So: **the rationale generator is a vision model reading a rendered PNG of the series, not a numeric tokenizer.** The rendered plot is a scaffold used only at data-generation time; the trained TSLM never sees the plot, only the raw numeric series through the encoder.

Generation config (verbatim):
- **GPT-4o snapshot: `gpt-4o-2024-08-06`**
- **Temperature: 0.3**
- **Seed: 42**

### The key trick: answer-set narrowing via a dissimilarity map

This is the single most transferable idea. Rather than asking GPT-4o to reason over all 8 classes (where it would guess badly), each generation call is turned into a **binary** discrimination between the **correct label** and a **deliberately dissimilar distractor** drawn from a hand-written mapping. GPT-4o is told both options and the correct answer is enforced via the required final line. This makes rationales grounded and non-degenerate. At training/eval time, the model faces the **full** label set — so the CoT is harder than the task it was distilled from is easy.

HAR dissimilarity map (correct → candidate distractors):
- Sitting → walking, running, biking, walking up, walking down
- Walking → sitting, lying, standing, biking, running
- Standing → walking, running, biking, walking up, walking down
- Running → sitting, lying, standing, biking, walking
- Walking up → sitting, lying, standing, biking, running
- Walking down → sitting, lying, standing, biking, running
- Lying → walking, running, biking, walking up, walking down
- Biking → sitting, lying, standing, walking, running

Sleep dissimilarity map: W → N3, N4, REM · N1 → W, N3, N4 · N2 → W, REM · N3 → W, REM · N4 → W, REM · REM → N2, N3, N4

ECG-QA: the two options come from the question template's own answer set (no hand map needed).

### The three CoT-generation prompts, VERBATIM

**A.2.1 — HAR-CoT**

```
You are shown a time-series plot of accelerometer over a 2.56 second window.
This data corresponds to one of two possible activities:
[CORRECT_ACTIVITY]
[DISSIMILAR_ACTIVITY]
Your task is to classify the activity based on analysis of the data.
Instructions:
- Begin by analyzing the time-series without assuming a specific label.
- Think step-by-step about what the observed patterns suggest regarding movement intensity and behavior.
- Write your rationale as a single, natural paragraph, do not use bullet
points, numbered steps, or section headings.
- Do not refer back to the plot or to the act of visual analysis in your rationale; the plot is only for reference but you should reason about the
time-series data.
- Do **not** assume any answer at the beginning, analyze as if you do not
yet know which class is correct.
- Do **not** mention either class label until the final sentence.
- Make sure that your last word is the answer. You MUST end your response
with "Answer: [CORRECT_ACTIVITY]":
```

**A.2.2 — SleepEDF-CoT**

```
You are presented with a time-series plot showing EEG data collected over a 30-second interval. This signal corresponds to one of two possible sleep stages:
- [SLEEP_STAGE_1]
- [SLEEP_STAGE_2]
Your task is to determine the correct sleep stage based solely on the observed patterns in the time series.
Instructions:
- Analyze the data objectively without presuming a particular label.
- Reason carefully and methodically about what the signal patterns suggest
regarding sleep stage.
- Write your reasoning as a single, coherent paragraph. Do not use bullet points, lists, or section headers.
- Do not reference the plot, visuals, or the process of viewing the data in your explanation; focus only on the characteristics of the time series.
- Do not mention or speculate about either class during the rationale, only reveal the correct class at the very end.
- Never state that you are uncertain or unable to classify the data. You must always provide a rationale and a final answer.
- Your final sentence must conclude with: "Answer: [CORRECT_SLEEP_STAGE]"
```

**A.2.3 — ECG-QA-CoT**

```
You are presented with a complete 12-lead ECG recording showing all standard leads (I, II, III, aVR, aVL, aVF, V1, V2, V3, V4, V5, V6).
Clinical Context: [CLINICAL_CONTEXT]
Question: [QUESTION]
This question has one of two possible answers:
- [ANSWER_OPTION_1]
- [ANSWER_OPTION_2]
Your task is to analyze the ECG and determine the correct answer based on the observed cardiac patterns. You may include the clinical context in your analysis if it helps you determine the correct answer.
Instructions:
- Analyze the ECG systematically without presuming a particular answer.
- Consider rhythm, rate, morphology, intervals, and any abnormalities you observe across all 12 leads.
- Think step-by-step about what the ECG patterns indicate regarding the clinical question above.
- Write your reasoning as a single, coherent paragraph. Do not use bullet points, lists, or section headers.
- Do not reference the visual aspects of viewing the ECG plot; focus on the cardiac characteristics and clinical significance.
- Do not mention or assume either answer option during your rationale, only reveal the correct answer at the very end.
- NEVER state uncertainty or inability to determine the answer. You MUST always provide clinical reasoning and a definitive answer.
- Your final sentence must conclude with: "Answer: [CORRECT_ANSWER]"
```

**A.3 — M4 captioning prompt (stage-1 pretraining), verbatim, and it is one line:**

```
Generate a detailed caption for the following time-series data:
```
with "a high-resolution plot, whose aspect ratio scales with sequence length to preserve visual fidelity and contextual detail." Trailing zero padding was truncated after the last non-zero element.

### The five recurring prompt-engineering constraints (portable to any domain)

1. Binary/narrowed answer set with a deliberately dissimilar distractor.
2. "Do not assume any answer at the beginning."
3. **"Do not refer back to the plot or the act of visual analysis"** — strips the visual scaffold out of the text so the rationale is valid for a model that only sees numbers.
4. Single natural paragraph, no bullets/headings.
5. Hard output contract: `&lt;rationale&gt; Answer: &lt;label&gt;` — the label must be the last token. Plus "never state uncertainty."

### Source data per dataset

| Dataset | Source | Processing |
|---|---|---|
| HAR-CoT | Merge of **DaLiAc, DOMINO, HHAR, PAMAP2, RealWorld, + Shoaib 2013/2014/2016**; only classes present in *all* datasets retained | Triaxial accel (X/Y/Z), **2.56 s windows @ 50 Hz**, 8 classes: sitting, walking, standing, running, walking up, walking down, lying, biking |
| Sleep-CoT | **Sleep-EDF** polysomnography, expert-annotated | **30 s windows, Fpz-Cz EEG channel only, 100 Hz**, 5 stages W/N1/N2/N3/REM (N3+N4 merged in main text; appendix table still lists N4 separately) |
| ECG-QA-CoT | **PTB-XL** signals + **ECG-QA** question templates | 12-lead, **10 s @ 100 Hz**. **Comparison questions excluded → 42 of 70 templates retained → 3,138 unique questions across ~240k samples** |

Splits: **80/10/10** for everything.

| Stage | Dataset | Train / Val / Test | #series | Length | Freq |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | TSQA | 38,400 / 4,800 / 4,800 | 1 | hours→years | n/s |
| 1 | M4-Captions | 80,000 / 10,000 / 10,000 | 1 | 64–512 pts | n/s |
| 2 | HAR-CoT | 68,542 / 8,718 / 8,222 | 3 | 2.56 s | 50 Hz |
| 2 | Sleep-CoT | 7,434 / 930 / 930 | 1 | 30 s | 100 Hz |
| 2 | ECG-QA-CoT | 159,313 / 31,137 / 41,093 | 12 | 10 s | 100 Hz |

(Per-class distributions: HAR train is sitting-heavy at 27.7%; Sleep train is Wake-heavy at 45.3%.)

### Validation of the recipe

- Prompts were "carefully engineered" and a **subset manually reviewed**.
- Downstream expert eval: **5 cardiologists**, 2 samples per template (**84 total**), each reviewed by ≥2 reviewers, rubric derived from ACC/AHA Clinical Competence Statement on ECGs + the **RIME** framework, scoring (1) correct ECG feature identification, (2) connection of features to the answer, (3) patient-context integration. Result: correct or partially correct in **92.9%**; context integration 85.1% positive, pattern recognition 65.5%, clinical reasoning 62.5%.
- **Stated limitation:** "we generated CoT datasets using GPT-4o on plots, which we have shown to perform poorly on these plots alone. Curated datasets likely lead to better rationales."

---

## Architecture

Two variants, both = time-series encoder + LLM.

**Encoder (shared).** Either **PatchTST** trained from scratch, or a frozen pretrained TSFM (**Chronos-2**, `https://huggingface.co/amazon/chronos-2`) with only a trainable projection layer. PatchTST path: split `x ∈ ℝ^L` into **non-overlapping** patches of size `p` → `N = L/p` patches; each patch → embedding via **1D convolution with kernel size = stride = p** → add **learnable positional encoding**. Then:
- OpenTSLM-SP: `TransformerEncoder`
- OpenTSLM-Flamingo: `PerceiverResampler` → fixed-size `Z_latent ∈ ℝ^{N_latent × d_time}`

**OpenTSLM-SoftPrompt (SP).** 3 components: encoder → **MLP projector** to `d_llm` → pretrained LLM fine-tuned with **LoRA**. Formally `Z = MLP(TransformerEncoder(E_{1:N})) ∈ ℝ^{N × d_llm}`. Input sequence:

```
X_input = [T_pre, Z_1, T_desc1, Z_2, T_desc2, …, Z_K, T_descK, T_post]
```
Each `(Z_i, T_desc_i)` is a "text–time-series chunk."

**OpenTSLM-Flamingo.** 4 components: patch encoder → PerceiverResampler → gated cross-attention layers → **frozen** LLM backbone. Gate: `Gated-XAttn(x, Z_latent) = x + γ · softmax(Q_text K_ts^T / √d_k) V_ts`, `γ` learnable. Two **learned special tokens** `&lt;TS&gt;` and `&lt;endofchunk&gt;`:

```
X_input = [pre_prompt, &lt;TS&gt;, ts_desc_1, &lt;endofchunk&gt;, &lt;TS&gt;, ts_desc_2, &lt;endofchunk&gt;, post_prompt]
```

Trainable params: SP = encoder + MLP + LoRA. Flamingo = encoder + cross-attention only.

## Series length, normalization, count, textual description

- **Normalization:** PatchEncoder expects `x ∈ [−1, 1]`. In the released code it is actually **per-series z-scoring** (`(x − mean)/std`, `std` clamped to `min_std = 1e-6`), computed per axis/channel.
- **Scale is not thrown away — it is re-injected as text.** Paper: *"we preserve scale and temporal context by adding the original mean, standard deviation, and time scale to the textual description. For example: `This is heart-rate data over 24 hours sampled at 50 Hz with mean=61 and std=12`."*
- **Actual code templates** (`_get_text_time_series_prompt_list`):
  - HAR (3 series): `"The following is the accelerometer data on the {x|y|z}-axis, it has mean {mean:.4f} and std {std:.4f}:"`
  - Sleep (1 series): `"The following is the EEG time series, it has mean {mean:.4f} and std {std:.4f}:"`
  - Simulation: `"This is a time series with mean {series_mean} and std {series_std}."`
- **Series per sample:** 1 (TSQA, M4, Sleep), 3 (HAR), 12 (ECG). Simulation sweeps N = 1..5.
- **Length limits:** no architectural cap ("time-series of any length"); the limit is memory + LLM context. Simulation sweeps L = 10 / 100 / 1,000 / 10,000. **SP OOMs at L=10,000, N≥3** (Llama-1B: 2.6 GB → 29.5 GB from L=10 to L=10,000 at N=1; Gemma-1B up to ~165 GB at L=10,000, N=5). **Flamingo is flat**: Llama-1B ≈ 20.4–21.0 GB, Llama-3B ≈ 60.7–61.1 GB, Gemma-270M ≈ 5.7–6.4 GB, Gemma-1B ≈ 15.4–15.6 GB across the entire sweep. On the real datasets SP goes 4.4 GB (TSQA) → 87.1 GB (ECG); Flamingo stays 61–72 GB for Llama-3B. Gemma has 32k context vs Llama's 128k, so soft prompts exhaust Gemma quickly.

**This is the single most important design decision for your new domain:** if your series are long or numerous, use Flamingo, not SoftPrompt.

## Training setup

- **Base LLMs:** `meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B` (default), `meta-llama/Llama-3.2-3B`, `google/gemma-3-270m`, `google/gemma-3-1b-pt`. (Gemma-3-4B-pt used only as a fine-tuned plot baseline.)
- **Two-stage curriculum** (paper) / **five stages** (code `curriculum_learning.py --stages stage1_mcq stage2_captioning stage3_cot stage4_sleep_cot stage5_ecg_cot`). Stage 1 = encoder warm-up on synthetic **TSQA** (MCQ) + **M4-Captions**; Stage 2 = the three CoT datasets. Each stage auto-loads the best checkpoint from the previous stage.
- **Objective:** standard next-token CE on the rationale; output contract `&lt;reasoning&gt; Answer: &lt;final answer&gt;`.
- **Hyperparameters:** AdamW; LR — SP: encoder 2e-4, LoRA 2e-4, projector 1e-4; Flamingo: encoder 2e-4, cross-attn 2e-4. Linear schedule, **10% warmup**, grad clip ℓ2 = 1.0, weight decay 0.01, **up to 200 epochs, early stopping patience 5**. LR choices follow Chow et al. 2024.
- **Hardware:** the paper reports **peak VRAM** extensively (Fig. 4, Fig. 18, Table 11) but **never names a GPU model or cluster**. From the numbers (87 GB peak, &gt;100 GB "OOM" threshold), they were on ≥80 GB-class accelerators, likely H100/A100-80GB or larger. Treat exact hardware as unreported.

## Evaluation

**Tasks:** TSQA (MCQ), HAR-CoT, Sleep-CoT, ECG-QA-CoT, plus M4 captioning (qualitative).
**Metrics:** **macro-F1** and **accuracy**, parsed from the `Answer:` suffix. Plus a distribution-adjusted random baseline, VRAM profiling, and the cardiologist rubric eval.

**Baselines:** (1) text-only LLM with digit-tokenized series, zero-shot; (2) same, LoRA-fine-tuned; (3) GPT-4o with tokenized text; (4) GPT-4o with **plot images**; (5) Gemma3-4B with plots, zero-shot and fine-tuned.

Selected results (F1 / Acc):

| Task | OpenTSLM-SP (Llama-1B) | OpenTSLM-Flamingo (Llama-1B) | Best baseline |
|---|---|---|---|
| TSQA | 97.50 / 97.54 | 94.08 / 94.00 | 84.54 / 82.06 (tokenized FT); GPT-4o+plot 59.24 |
| HAR-CoT | 65.44 / 71.48 | 62.93 / 69.27 | 60.44 (tokenized FT); GPT-4o+plot 10.83 |
| Sleep-CoT | 69.88 / 81.08 | 49.33 / 67.31 | 9.05 (tokenized FT) |
| ECG-QA-CoT | 32.84 / 35.49 | 34.62 / 38.14 | 24.95 (GPT-4o) — tokenized FT **infeasible (OOM)** |

Chronos-2 as frozen encoder improves nearly everything: TSQA Gemma3-270M **77.86 → 99.69**; Sleep-CoT Llama-1B **49.33 → 64.57**, Gemma-270M **51.38 → 68.33**. Best reported ECG-QA-CoT is 40.25 F1.

## Verbatim example: prompt + series + answer

The paper's fully-transcribed literal example is the **HAR baseline failure case (A.4.1)**, which shows exactly how a sample is serialized (note the deliberately mangled digit-tokenization used for text baselines — digits are space-separated and the decimal point stripped):

```
You are given accelerometer data in all three dimensions. Your task is
to classify the activity based on analysis of the data.
Instructions:
- Begin by analyzing the time series without assuming a specific label.
- Think step-by-step about what the observed patterns suggest regarding movement intensity and behavior.
- Write your rationale as a single, natural paragraph, do not use bullet points, numbered steps, or section headings.
- Do **not** mention any class label until the final sentence.
The following is the accelerometer data on the x-axis, it has mean -3.2434 and std 0.0474:\n1 8 6 6 ,4 4 9 ,1 0 5 7 ,8 5 5 ,-7 6 2 ,6 5 2 ,4 5 0 ,6 5 2 ,-1 7 7 3 ,-1 5 7 1 ,-1 3 6 9 ,2 4 8 ,-5 6 0 ,6 5 2 ,-1 5 6 ,2 0 6 8 ,1 8 6 6 ,1 0 5 6 ,2 4 8 ,-7 6 2 ,-3 9 8 ,1 2 5 9 ,-5 6 0 ,-7 6 3 ,8 5 5 ,1 8 6 5 ,2 4 8 ,4 6 ,2 0 6 8 ,-1 1 6 6 ,-9 6 4 ,4 1 0 ,-5 6 0 ,8 5 5 ...
The following is the accelerometer data on the y-axis, it has mean 2.3132 and std 0.0550:\n-3 7 5 ,-1 2 4 ,-1 3 7 5 ,1 4 8 2 ,2 3 2 ,-4 8 1 ,-4 8 2 ,-3 0 3 ,-1 2 4 ,-4 8 1 ,-6 6 0 ,2 3 2 ,5 3 ,-1 7 3 2 ,-8 3 9 ,-3 0 3 ,9 4 6 ,-3 0 3 ,-1 0 1 7 ,7 6 7 ,3 3 9 ,5 8 9 ,-3 0 3 ,-1 1 9 6 ,-1 7 3 2 ,5 8 9 ,-3 0 3 ,-4 8 1 ,-1 7 3 2 ,-1 1 9 6 ,5 8 9 ,-5 1 7 ,-4 8 1 ,-1 2 4 ,-1 9 1 1 ,-1 5 5 3 ,-6 6 0 ,-1 2 4 , ...
The following is the accelerometer data on the z-axis, it has mean 9.2017 and std 0.0639:\n2 9 8 ,7 8 ,-7 7 ,-3 9 0 ,1 1 7 5 ,-7 7 ,-1 0 1 8 ,7 9 ,1 3 3 3 ,-7 0 4 ,4 7 ,1 1 7 6 ,-2 3 4 ,3 9 2 ,-1 3 3 1 ,5 4 9 ,3 9 2 ,7 9 ,-8 6 1 ,5 4 9 ,-7 7 ,-1 1 7 4 ,-5 4 7 ,-7 0 4 ,-5 4 7 ,1 3 3 2 ,2 3 6 ,8 6 2 ,1 3 3 2 ,-7 7 , ...
Possible activity labels are:
biking, lying, running, sitting, standing, walking, walking_down, walking_up.
- Please now write your rationale. Make sure that your last word is the answer. You MUST end your response with "Answer:"
```

The corresponding Sleep baseline prompt (A.4.2) is identical in shape with a single `The following is the EEG time series, it has mean … and std …:` block and the label list `Wake, Non-REM stage 1, Non-REM stage 2, Non-REM stage 3, REM sleep, Movement`.

### The training-time prompts (from the released code — this is what you actually reimplement)

`HARCoTQADataset` pre-prompt (verbatim):
```
You are given accelerometer data in all three dimensions. Your task is to classify the activity based on analysis of the data.

Instructions:
- Begin by analyzing the time series without assuming a specific label.
- Think step-by-step about what the observed patterns suggest regarding movement intensity and behavior.
- Write your rationale as a single, natural paragraph — do not use bullet points, numbered steps, or section headings.
- Do **not** mention any class label until the final sentence.

Possible activity labels are:
biking, lying, running, sitting, standing, walking, walking_down, walking_up.

- Make sure that your last word is the answer. You MUST end your response with "Answer: "
```
post-prompt: `Rationale:` · answer field: `row["rationale"]`

`SleepEDFCoTQADataset` post-prompt (verbatim):
```
Possible sleep stages are:
Wake, Non-REM stage 1, Non-REM stage 2, Non-REM stage 3, REM sleep, Movement

- Please now write your rationale. Make sure that your last word is the answer. You MUST end your response with "Answer: 
```

`ECGQACoTQADataset` pre-prompt (verbatim): `You are an expert cardiologist analyzing an ECG (electrocardiogram). \n\nClinical Context: {clinical_context}\n\nYour task is to examine the ECG signal and answer the following medical question:\n\nQuestion: {question}\n\nInstructions:\n- Begin by analyzing the time series without assuming a specific answer.\n- Think step-by-step …` and post-prompt `Based on your analysis of the ECG data, select your answer from the following options:\n{answers_text}\n\n- Make sure that your last word is the answer. You MUST end your response with "Answer: "` (options looked up per `template_id`).

A real ECG clinical-context string, verbatim from `src/opentslm/time_series_datasets/ecg_qa/example_prompt.md`:
&gt; **Clinical Context:** 76-year-old male patient. 12-lead ECG. clinical recording. recorded with AT-6 6. Signal quality: baseline drift noted, static noise present, burst noise present, electrode artifacts present. extra beats detected during recording. pacemaker present.
&gt; **Question:** Does this ECG show symptoms of non-diagnostic t abnormalities?
&gt; **Expected Answer:** yes

### Replication checklist for a new domain

1. Get labelled windows. Fix window length + sampling rate.
2. Hand-write a **dissimilarity map** label → maximally-confusable-but-distinct distractors.
3. Render each sample to one figure, **unnormalized**, one subplot per channel.
4. Call `gpt-4o-2024-08-06`, temp 0.3, seed 42, with the template above adapted: state both options, ban plot references, ban uncertainty, demand one paragraph, demand `Answer: [CORRECT]` last.
5. Manually review a subset; regenerate failures.
6. Store `{series_per_channel, label, rationale}`. Split 80/10/10.
7. At training time: z-score per channel, emit `"The following is the {channel} data, it has mean {m:.4f} and std {s:.4f}:"` per channel, list the **full** label set in the pre/post-prompt, target = rationale ending `Answer: &lt;label&gt;`.
8. Warm the encoder on stage-1 synthetic MCQ + captioning before the CoT stage.

# PART B — Deutsche Bahn open data

**Universal base URL for all DB API Marketplace APIs:**
`https://apis.deutschebahn.com/db-api-marketplace/apis/…`
**Auth headers (all products):**
```
DB-Client-Id:  &lt;client id&gt;
DB-Api-Key:    &lt;api key&gt;
Accept:        application/xml, application/json
```
(Legacy host `https://api.deutschebahn.com/&lt;product&gt;/v2` still appears in the OpenAPI specs and works for StaDa/FaSta.)

## 1. Timetables API (IRIS)

- **Product page:** https://developers.deutschebahn.com/db-api-marketplace/apis/product/timetables
- **Endpoints:**
  - `GET /timetables/v1/plan/{evaNo}/{YYMMDD}/{HH}` — planned stops for one station, one **hourly slice**. Contains **only** planned attributes; no delays, no platform changes.
  - `GET /timetables/v1/fchg/{evaNo}` — **full changes**: all currently-known deviations for that station. Updated ~every 30 s. Changes are **purged once the event is in the past** (empirically &lt;1 h retention).
  - `GET /timetables/v1/rchg/{evaNo}` — **recent changes**: only changes modified in the **last 2 minutes**. Designed for tight polling loops.
  - `GET /timetables/v1/station/{pattern}` — station lookup by name/EVA/DS100.
- **Auth:** register at developers.deutschebahn.com → create an application → subscribe to the free Timetables plan. **API key required.**
- **Rate limit:** free plan = **60 calls/minute**. (piebro's production scraper configures `rate_limit=1000`/min with `max_concurrent=10`, implying higher limits are grantable.)
- **Format:** XML (`Accept: application/xml`).
- **Horizon / history:** **~18 hours forward only.** **History is NOT served** — `fchg` drops past events and `plan` is not a historical archive. This is precisely why piebro and Bahn-Vorhersage exist: you must poll continuously and archive yourself.
- **Licence:** CC BY 4.0, attribute Deutsche Bahn AG.

**XML schema**

| Element | Attributes |
|---|---|
| `&lt;timetable&gt;` | `station` (name), `eva` (EVA number) |
| `&lt;s&gt;` (stop) | `id` — unique stop id, the join key between `plan` and `fchg`/`rchg` output; `eva` (present in change data) |
| `&lt;tl&gt;` (trip label) | `f` = distance class: `F` long-distance / `N` short-distance / `S` regional; `t` = trip type (usually `p`); `o` = operator (EVU) code; `c` = train category (ICE, IC, RE, RB, S…); `n` = train number (Zugnummer) |
| `&lt;ar&gt;` / `&lt;dp&gt;` | see below |
| `&lt;m&gt;` (message) | see below |
| `&lt;hd&gt;`, `&lt;hpc&gt;`, `&lt;conn&gt;`, `&lt;rtr&gt;` | historic delay, historic platform change, connection, ref-trip relation — present in the XSD, sparsely populated |

`&lt;ar&gt;` / `&lt;dp&gt;` attributes — **planned** set appears in `/plan`, **changed** set appears in `/fchg` and `/rchg`:

| Attr | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `pt` | **planned time**, format `yyMMddHHmm`, German local time |
| `ct` | **changed time** — the actual/estimated time. `delay = ct − pt` |
| `pp` | planned platform (Gleis) |
| `cp` | changed platform |
| `ppth` | **planned path** — `|`-separated list of station names before (for `ar`) / after (for `dp`) this stop |
| `cpth` | changed path |
| `l` | **line** designation (e.g. `S32`, `RB23`); null for long-distance |
| `ps` | planned status |
| `cs` | **changed status**: `P` = planned, `A` = added, `C` = cancelled |
| `clt` | **cancellation time** — when the event was cancelled (train should be hidden from displays after this) |
| `hi` | **hidden** flag (1 = do not show to passengers) |
| `wings` | `|`-separated ids of wing trains |
| `tra` | transition — trip id this journey continues into |
| `pde` / `cde` | planned / changed **destination** |
| `dc` | distant change flag |

`&lt;m&gt;` (message) attributes: `id` (unique), `t` (type — `h` HIM/system, `q` quality-of-service, `f` free text, `d` delay, `c` cause, `r` reason), **`c`** (**cause code**, the numeric code), `cat` (German display category), `pr` (priority, `1` HIGHEST → `4` DONE), `ts` (creation timestamp), `from` / `to` (validity window).

**Cause codes `m@c` — full documented list** (source: derf's `Travel::Status::DE::IRIS::Result`, the de-facto reference; DB does not publish this table publicly). Codes **1–69 and 99 = delay causes**, **70–98 = quality-of-service (QoS)**:

99 Verzögerungen im Betriebsablauf

Codes **91, 93, 95, 83, 96** are the accessibility-relevant ones; **97** is the crowding signal.

## 2. piebro / deutsche-bahn-data — **the best instant-start option**

- **Repo:** https://github.com/piebro/deutsche-bahn-data (code MIT)
- **HF dataset id:** `piebro/deutsche-bahn-data` → https://huggingface.co/datasets/piebro/deutsche-bahn-data
- **Stats:** **225.8 M rows, 67.6 GB, Parquet, CC BY 4.0** (attribute Deutsche Bahn). ~6.4k downloads/month.
- **Partitioning:**
  - `monthly_processed_data/data-YYYY-MM.parquet` — one file per month, this is what you want.
  - `raw_data/year={year}/month={month}/day={day}/…` — hive-partitioned raw API responses, hourly.
- **Coverage:** biggest **~100 stations from 2024-07** through **2025-11-02**; **all stations from 2025-11-02 to present**. Known gaps: 2025-11 6 h, 2026-02 7 h, 2026-03 10 h, 2026-04 34 h, 2026-05 31 h, 2026-06 6 h, 2026-07 102 h. All timestamps **Europe/Berlin (CET/CEST)**, no conversion applied.
- **Collection:** a GitHub Actions cron runs **4×/day**, snapped to fixed 6-hour blocks. It calls **StaDa** (`station-data/v2/stations`) for EVA numbers, then **`timetables/v1/plan/{eva}/{date}/{hour}`** per station-hour and **`timetables/v1/fchg/{eva}`** per station (reaching back &gt;6 h), plus one `fasta/v2/facilities` snapshot.
- **The 17 monthly columns (+`id`):**

| Column | Type | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `station_name` | string | |
| `xml_station_name` | string | name as it appears in the XML |
| `eva` | string | EVA number |
| `train_number` | string | Zugnummer, raw `tl.n` |
| `line_number` | string | Liniennummer, raw `ar.l`/`dp.l`; **null for ICE/IC/EC** |
| `final_destination_station` | string | |
| `delay_in_min` | integer | |
| `time` | timestamp | actual arrival or departure time |
| `arrival_is_canceled` | boolean | |
| `departure_is_canceled` | boolean | |
| `train_type` | string | ICE, IC, RE… |
| `train_line_ride_id` | string | unique id for the train ride |
| `train_line_station_num` | integer | stop index within the route |
| `arrival_planned_time` | timestamp | `ar.pt` |
| `arrival_change_time` | timestamp | `ar.ct` |
| `departure_planned_time` | timestamp | `dp.pt` |
| `departure_change_time` | timestamp | `dp.ct` |
| `id` | string | unique train-stop id |

  Schema note: pre-2026-08 files had a single `is_canceled`; reconstruct with `df["is_canceled"] = df["arrival_is_canceled"] | df["departure_is_canceled"]`. Pre-2026-05 had `train_name`; reconstruct with `train_type + " " + train_number`.
- **Raw parquet columns:** `timestamp`, `url`, `api_name` (e.g. `timetables/v1/plan`, `timetables/v1/fchg`), `query_params` (JSON string), `response_data` (**the raw XML/JSON body** — this is where `ppth`, `m@c` cause codes, `hi`, `clt` etc. survive; the monthly files drop them), `status_code`, `error`, `duration_ms`, `year`, `month`, `day`.
- **Download — no credentials needed:**
```bash
uv run --with "huggingface-hub" hf download piebro/deutsche-bahn-data \
  --repo-type=dataset --local-dir=. --include "monthly_processed_data/*"
# everything incl. raw:
uv run --with "huggingface-hub" hf download piebro/deutsche-bahn-data --repo-type=dataset --local-dir=.
```
  Or stream a single month directly with polars/duckdb:
  `https://huggingface.co/datasets/piebro/deutsche-bahn-data/resolve/main/monthly_processed_data/data-2026-08.parquet`
- Stats site: https://piebro.github.io/deutsche-bahn-data/stats/allgemein.html

## 3. Bahn-Vorhersage

- **Site:** https://bahnvorhersage.de · open-data index at `/open-data/` · code at **https://gitlab.com/bahnvorhersage/bahnvorhersage** (GPL-3.0+), org at https://github.com/TrAInConnectionPrediction
- **Data paper:** Theo Döllmann, *"Bahn-Vorhersage dataset: an open archive of most train delays in Germany since September 2021"*, Universität Augsburg preprint 2025, https://opus.bibliothek.uni-augsburg.de/opus4/frontdoor/index/index/docId/127213 (PDF `/opus4/files/127213/paper_doellmann.pdf`)
- **Licence:** **ODbL**, attribution to *Bahn-Vorhersage, Deutsche Bahn, Trainline, DELFI*.
- **How obtained:** continuous per-station polling of the **DB Timetables API** since **September 2021**; XML converted 1:1 to JSON. Augmented with **trip length from OpenStreetMap**, then flattened to a tabular ML-ready format.

Three published products, all on **Mobilithek (login required)**:

**(a) Geparste Verspätungen** — https://bahnvorhersage.de/open-data/parsed-train-delays/ → https://mobilithek.info/offers/938616012299546624
Yearly `.tar` → daily **Parquet**, grouped by `service_date`. **One row per stop event** (arrival and departure are separate rows; filter on `is_arrival`). **Temporal resolution: one minute** ("Die Daten haben eine zeitliche Auflösung / Genauigkeit von einer Minute"). **Forecast tracking since September 2023** — i.e. from Sept 2023 the archive retains the *sequence of successive DB real-time forecasts* per stop event, each row stamped with `update_timestamp`, not just the final value. To reconstruct the per-stop one-minute forecast trajectory: group by (`trip_id`, `stop_id`, `is_arrival`) and order by `update_timestamp`; `is_final == true` marks the settled value.
Columns (~37): `trip_id` Int64 (hashed), `time_schedule`, `time_real`, `update_timestamp`, `delay` Int32 (**seconds**), `dwell_time_schedule`, `dwell_time_real`, `is_final`, `is_arrival`, `is_cancelled`, `is_regional`, `category` (ICE/RE/RB/S), `number`, `line`, `operator`, `stop_id` Int64, `platform_id_schedule`, `platform_id_real`, `lat`, `lon` (EPSG:4326), `distance_traveled` Int32 (metres from origin), `initial_scheduled_departure`, `initial_stop_id`, `stop_sequence` UInt16 (1-based), `trip_headsign` (destination stop id), `is_betriebshalt`, `pickup_drop_off_type` enum (REGULAR=0 / NOT_ALLOWED=1), `message_codes` List(Int16) — **these are the IRIS `m@c` cause codes from the table above**. Timestamps UTC, microsecond precision (source truncates seconds).

**(b) Rohdaten** — https://bahnvorhersage.de/open-data/raw-data/ → schedule https://mobilithek.info/offers/938849406229053440 · realtime https://mobilithek.info/offers/938859748233478144
Yearly `.tar` of **Brotli-compressed daily CSV**. Schedule dump columns: `stop_id` (Int, EVA), `plan` (JSON string). Realtime dump: `stop_id`, `time_crawled` (ISO 8601), `change` (JSON string).

**(c) Stop Data** — https://bahnvorhersage.de/open-data/stops/ → https://mobilithek.info/offers/940263406913355776
`stops.parquet` (GTFS-compatible, joins to `stop_id` in the delay dataset) and `stations.parquet`. Built from DB **RIS::Stations**, HAFAS, `Travel-Status-DE-IRIS` lists, Trainline's DB, the German **zHV** central stop directory, and manual entries; includes historical changes.

## 4. StaDa (Station Data)

- **Product:** https://developers.deutschebahn.com/db-api-marketplace/apis/product/stada · OpenAPI v2.2.01 "Stationsdatenbereitstellung"
- **Servers:** `https://api.deutschebahn.com/stada/v2` · marketplace form: `https://apis.deutschebahn.com/db-api-marketplace/apis/station-data/v2`
- **Endpoints:** `GET /stations` · `GET /stations/{id}` (id = Bahnhofsnummer) · `GET /szentralen` · `GET /szentralen/{id}`
- **Query params on `/stations`:** `offset`, `limit` (max &amp; default 10000), `searchstring` (supports `*` and `?` wildcards, comma lists), `category` (1–7, ranges like `2-4` or `1,3-5`), `federalstate` (comma lists), `eva`, `ril` (Ril100/DS100), `logicaloperator` (`and`|`or`). Umlauts must be percent-encoded. Repeated params are ANDed unless `logicaloperator=or`.
- **Auth:** API key required. Throttling tier in the spec is "Unlimited", but DB documents it as **static data — call at most once per day per key**.
- **Format:** JSON. Envelope `StationQuery { total, limit, offset, result[] }`.
- **`Station` object — full field list:**
  `number` (int32, Bahnhofsnummer — the join key to FaSta `stationnumber`), `name`, `category` (int32, −1…7; −1/0 = not in production), `priceCategory` (1..7), `federalState`, `evaNumbers[]` → `{ number (EVA int), isMain (bool), geographicCoordinate: GeoJSON Point [lon, lat] }`, `riL100Identifiers[]` → `{ rilIdentifier, isMain, hasSteamPermission, geographicCoordinate }`, `mailingAdress { street, houseNumber, zipcode, city }`, `aufgabentraeger { name, shortname }`, `regionalbereich { number, name, shortName }`, `stationManagement { number, name }`, `szentrale { number, name, publicPhoneNumber }`, `timetableOffice { name, email }`, `DBinformation` (Schedule), `localServiceStaff` (Schedule).
  `Schedule` = `{ monday…sunday, holiday }`, each an `OpeningHours { fromTime, toTime }`.
- **Accessibility / amenity flags** (all boolean unless noted):
  **`hasSteplessAccess`** — enum **`yes` | `no` | `partial`** (not a boolean!)
  **`hasMobilityService`** — **string**, values `"no"`, `"yes, advance notification is requested…"`, `"yes, advance notification is required…"`
  `hasPublicFacilities` (toilets), `hasWiFi`, `hasParking`, `hasBicycleParking`, `hasCarRental`, `hasTaxiRank`, `hasLocalPublicTransport`, `hasDBLounge`, `hasLockerSystem`, `hasLostAndFound`, `hasRailwayMission`, `hasTravelCenter`, `hasTravelNecessities`
- Coordinates arrive as GeoJSON `coordinates: [longitude, latitude]` (lon first).

## 5. FaSta (Facility Status)

- **Product:** https://developers.deutschebahn.com/db-api-marketplace/apis/product/fasta · OpenAPI v2.1 (service v2.1.423), operated by DB InfraGO AG
- **Server:** `https://api.deutschebahn.com/fasta/v2` · marketplace: `https://apis.deutschebahn.com/db-api-marketplace/apis/fasta/v2`
- **Endpoints:**
  - `GET /facilities` — params: `type` (array: `ESCALATOR`, `ELEVATOR`), `state` (array: `ACTIVE`, `INACTIVE`, `UNKNOWN`), `equipmentnumbers` (array), `stationnumber` (int), `area` (4 numbers: longitudeWest, latitudeSouth, longitudeEast, latitudeNorth, WGS84)
  - `GET /facilities/{equipmentnumber}`
  - `GET /stations/{stationnumber}` → `station { stationnumber, name, facilities[] }`
- **Auth:** API key. Free plan "Free4All", **60 calls/second**, 24/7 but no SLA.
- **Format:** JSON. **`facility` fields:** `equipmentnumber` (int, unique), `type` (`ESCALATOR`|`ELEVATOR`), `description` (free text location, e.g. "zu Gleis 3/4"), `state` (`ACTIVE`|`INACTIVE`|`UNKNOWN`), `stateExplanation` (string), `geocoordX` (**longitude**), `geocoordY` (**latitude**), `stationnumber` (int → joins to StaDa `Station.number`), `operatorname`.
- **History: LIVE ONLY.** No historical endpoint. Two archives exist: **https://github.com/defgsus/bahn-api-history** (JSON change-logs, **3,894 elevators/escalators, 17,278 snapshots, Jan 2020 – Sep 2022**; also 288 parking locations / 73,942 snapshots and 5,406 stations / 910 snapshots), and the `raw_data` partitions of piebro's HF dataset (one `fasta/v2/facilities` snapshot per fetch run, 4×/day since 2024-07).

## 6. Wagenreihung (coach sequence)

- Available on the DB API Marketplace as a Fahrzeugreihenfolge product ("Wagenreihung"): carriage order, **wagon-precise positioning on the platform**, united/combined journeys, and per-wagon equipment features. Product slugs `…/product/wagenreihung` and `…/product/ist-wagenreihung` both 404'd during this research — the product is reachable only from the authenticated product catalogue at https://developers.deutschebahn.com/db-api-marketplace/apis/product, and modern access is via the **RIS** family (`RIS::Journeys`, `RIS::Boards`, `RIS::Stations`, `RIS::Connections`, `RIS::Disruptions`), which are **partner/contract APIs, not Free4All**.
- **Response shape** (from client libraries): `initializationDate`, `product` (e.g. `"ICE"`), `trainNumber`, `serviceId`, `wagons[]` → `{ group, type (e.g. TRIEBKOPF, REISEZUGWAGENERSTEKLASSE), id, wagonNumber (or null), status (e.g. OFFEN), fahrzeugsektor / platform sector }`. German-language variants of the same fields: `fahrzeuggruppebezeichnung`, `fahrzeugnummer`, `fahrzeugtyp`, `fahrzeugsektor`.
- **Client libraries** (all now archived/deprecated — the underlying unofficial endpoint has churned): `juliuste/db-wagenreihung` (JS, archived 2025-04-06), `MrKrisKrisu/db-wagenreihung-php` (archived 2025-04-29), `derf/Travel-Status-DE-DBWagenreihung` (Perl, deprecated → use `Travel::Status::DE::DBRIS`).
- **Verdict: do not build a hackathon dependency on this.** Coverage is long-distance only (ICE/IC/EC), current day only, no history.

## 7. GTFS

**Instant, no-credential option — gtfs.de** (feeds derived from the DELFI NeTEx dataset, regenerated **daily**, cover the **next 30 days**, licence **CC BY 4.0**):

| Feed | Download URL | Size | Trips | Stops |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Full Germany | `https://download.gtfs.de/germany/free/latest.zip` | 272 MB | 1.8 M | 674 K |
| Long-distance rail | `https://download.gtfs.de/germany/fv_free/latest.zip` | 404 KB | 5.4 K | 1.3 K |
| Regional rail | `https://download.gtfs.de/germany/rv_free/latest.zip` | 11 MB | 100 K | 17 K |
| Local transit (ÖPNV) | `https://download.gtfs.de/germany/nv_free/latest.zip` | 261 MB | 1.7 M | 662 K |

**GTFS-Realtime for Germany:** `https://realtime.gtfs.de/realtime-free.pb` — **protobuf, free, NO registration, NO API key**, updated **every 10 s**, **CC BY-SA 4.0**. Contains TripUpdates and ServiceAlerts. Aggregates VVO, VRS, VBN, VRR, NVBW, VAG, VBB, Bayern and other DELFI SIRI feeds plus CH/NL border regions. Built under the BMDV project "DEEZ – Deutschlandweite Echtzeitdaten"; also offered as SIRI-ET.

**Official DELFI source (registration required):**
- Mobilithek: https://mobilithek.info/offers/-2883874086141693018 ("Deutschlandweite Sollfahrplandaten (GTFS)")
- OpenData-ÖPNV: https://www.opendata-oepnv.de/ (dataset `deutschlandweite-sollfahrplandaten-gtfs`) — download button reads *"Bitte Anmelden"*; **registration mandatory**.
- Licence CC BY. Published **Mondays** (next business day if a Hessian public holiday). Also available as NeTEx and zHV.

**Columns that matter for accessibility/routing work:**
- `routes.txt`: `route_type` (**2 = Rail**, 0 tram, 1 subway, 3 bus, 100–117 extended rail types), `route_id`, `route_short_name`, `route_long_name`, `agency_id`
- `trips.txt`: `trip_id`, `route_id`, `service_id`, `trip_headsign`, `direction_id`, `block_id`, `shape_id`, **`wheelchair_accessible`** (0 unknown / 1 accessible / 2 not), **`bikes_allowed`** (0 unknown / 1 allowed / 2 not)
- `stop_times.txt`: `trip_id`, `arrival_time`, `departure_time`, `stop_id`, `stop_sequence`, `stop_headsign`, `pickup_type`, `drop_off_type`, `shape_dist_traveled`, `timepoint`
- `stops.txt`: `stop_id`, `stop_code`, `stop_name`, `stop_lat`, `stop_lon`, `location_type`, `parent_station`, **`wheelchair_boarding`** (0/1/2), **`platform_code`**, `zone_id`, `stop_timezone`, `level_id`
- `transfers.txt`: `from_stop_id`, `to_stop_id`, `transfer_type` (0 recommended / 1 timed / 2 min-time-required / 3 not possible), **`min_transfer_time`** (seconds), plus `from_trip_id`/`to_trip_id` in newer specs
- `calendar.txt`: `service_id`, `monday`…`sunday`, `start_date`, `end_date` · `calendar_dates.txt`: `service_id`, `date`, `exception_type` (1 added / 2 removed)
- `agency.txt`, `shapes.txt`, and optionally `pathways.txt` / `levels.txt` (in-station walking graph incl. elevators — check presence per feed; the DELFI export is inconsistent here)

Note: `wheelchair_accessible` / `bikes_allowed` / `wheelchair_boarding` are **frequently `0` (unknown)** in the DELFI-derived feeds. StaDa's `hasSteplessAccess` + FaSta live elevator state are the higher-quality accessibility signal for German rail stations; join StaDa `evaNumbers[].number` ↔ GTFS `stop_id` (DELFI stop ids are DHID/IFOPT-style like `de:08111:6118`, so you generally need the **zHV** crosswalk or a lat/lon + name match).

---

## Credentials: blockers vs. instant

**Instantly downloadable, zero credentials — build the pipeline on these:**

| Source | URL |
|---|---|
| piebro HF dataset (225.8 M rows, 67.6 GB, incl. raw XML) | `huggingface.co/datasets/piebro/deutsche-bahn-data` |
| gtfs.de static GTFS (all four feeds) | `download.gtfs.de/germany/{free,fv_free,rv_free,nv_free}/latest.zip` |
| gtfs.de GTFS-Realtime protobuf | `realtime.gtfs.de/realtime-free.pb` |
| defgsus elevator/station/parking history (2020–2022) | `github.com/defgsus/bahn-api-history` |
| Bahn-Vorhersage data paper (schema reference) | `opus.bibliothek.uni-augsburg.de/opus4/files/127213/paper_doellmann.pdf` |
| IRIS cause-code table | `metacpan.org/dist/Travel-Status-DE-IRIS` (`Result.pm`) |

**Needs registration — start these immediately in parallel, they are not guaranteed within 24 h:**

| Source | Friction |
|---|---|
| **DB API Marketplace** (Timetables, StaDa, FaSta) | Account + create an "application" + subscribe per API. The Free4All plans are self-service and usually approve in minutes to a few hours, but it is a manual portal flow with email verification. **Medium risk.** |
| **Mobilithek** (all three Bahn-Vorhersage datasets, official DELFI GTFS) | Full account registration; some offers require an explicit data request/approval per offer. **High risk for a 24 h window — assume you will not get these in time.** |
| **opendata-oepnv.de** (official DELFI GTFS) | Registration required. Use gtfs.de instead. **Avoidable.** |
| **DB RIS / Wagenreihung** | Partner/contract APIs, not self-service. **Do not attempt.** |

**Recommended 24 h plan:** start the DB Marketplace registration in minute one (it is the only credentialed source worth waiting for, and it unlocks live `fchg`/`rchg` for a live demo). Meanwhile build everything on **piebro's HF parquet for history** (it already contains the raw IRIS XML, so you can recover `ppth`, `hi`, `clt` and the `m@c` cause codes yourself from `raw_data/`), **gtfs.de static** for the schedule graph and accessibility columns, and **realtime.gtfs.de** for a live layer that needs no key at all. Skip Mobilithek and Wagenreihung entirely.

Sources: [arXiv 2510.02410](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.02410) · [OpenTSLM HTML v3](https://arxiv.org/html/2510.02410v3) · [StanfordBDHG/OpenTSLM](https://github.com/StanfordBDHG/OpenTSLM) · [piebro/deutsche-bahn-data](https://github.com/piebro/deutsche-bahn-data) · [HF dataset](https://huggingface.co/datasets/piebro/deutsche-bahn-data) · [DB Timetables](https://developers.deutschebahn.com/db-api-marketplace/apis/product/timetables) · [StaDa](https://developers.deutschebahn.com/db-api-marketplace/apis/product/stada) · [FaSta](https://developers.deutschebahn.com/db-api-marketplace/apis/product/fasta) · [Travel::Status::DE::IRIS](https://metacpan.org/dist/Travel-Status-DE-IRIS) · [openHAB DB binding](https://www.openhab.org/addons/bindings/deutschebahn/) · [Bahn-Vorhersage open data](https://bahnvorhersage.de/open-data/parsed-train-delays/) · [Bahn-Vorhersage data paper](https://opus.bibliothek.uni-augsburg.de/opus4/frontdoor/index/index/docId/127213) · [gtfs.de](https://gtfs.de/) · [gtfs.de realtime](https://gtfs.de/de/realtime/) · [defgsus/bahn-api-history](https://github.com/defgsus/bahn-api-history)</result>

# 1. The design fork, resolved

## 1.1 Your hypothesis is right in shape and wrong in three details

Keep the frame: **a route is a sequence of legs, each leg grounded in dense hourly aggregates.** But three things in the current form need fixing before it survives contact with the deadline.

**Detail 1 — the load-bearing series is the line, not the station, and your hypothesis treats them as peers.** A Hbf's mean delay mixes S-Bahn, RE and ICE into one number. An ICE leg's risk is dominated by *that line's* upstream accumulation, not by the station average. The per-line hourly curve is the causal signal; the per-station curve is congestion context. Rank them accordingly, and drop station channels that only restate traffic.

**Detail 2 — 3 channels × 3 nodes + line series is ~11 series, and most of them are redundant.** Not a memory problem (11 × 168 → 462 patch tokens on OpenTSLMSP, fine on an L40S) but an *information* problem: cancel-rate and train-count at the origin and destination mostly re-encode the same daily traffic shape. Every series you add that no clause of the rationale references is a series the encoder must learn to ignore.

**Detail 3 — the destination needs no series at all.** Arriving late at B is the *outcome*, not a driver. Nothing at B causes the plan to fail. Cutting the destination node frees three slots and — importantly — removes B from the density constraint, which widens the itinerary pool enormously (see §1.5).

## 1.2 The reconciliation with the teammate's argument (this is the pitch line)

His objection is to **per-run sequences as model inputs**: 10–30 stops, irregular spacing, a text baseline matches it. That objection is correct and I would not overturn it.

But it says nothing about using run-level records to **compute the label**. The run-level join never enters the model — it produces a number. So:

&gt; **The run-level data becomes the label, not the series.** The series stay dense, hourly and seasonal exactly as he argued; the target becomes route-level exactly as the product question demands.

And the second half: a **per-line hourly curve** is just as dense and seasonal as a per-station one — an ICE line aggregated across all its stops per hour has tens of observations in daytime hours and a strong accumulate-through-the-day / reset-overnight cycle. So the route question is answerable *without ever making a series out of a single train run*. Say this explicitly in the pitch; it turns an apparent team disagreement into a designed decision.

## 1.3 Exact series per sample: six, fixed

One record = one **itinerary template** × one **168h context window**. Itineraries are restricted to exactly one transfer (two legs), which keeps N constant and puts the sample squarely on the interesting question.

| # | `signal` | Content | `spec_type` | `unit_value` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `board_station_delay` | Hourly mean delay of all trains at boarding station **A** | `delay` | `ureg.minute` |
| 2 | `board_line_delay` | Hourly mean delay of service group **L1**, across all its stops network-wide | `delay` | `ureg.minute` |
| 3 | `transfer_station_delay` | Hourly mean delay of all trains at transfer hub **X** | `delay` | `ureg.minute` |
| 4 | `transfer_station_cancel_rate` | Hourly cancellation rate at **X** | `cancellation-rate` | `ureg.dimensionless` |
| 5 | `connect_line_delay` | Hourly mean delay of service group **L2**, network-wide | `delay` | `ureg.minute` |
| 6 | `connect_line_service` | Hourly count of **L2** stop events (service level / thinning) | `throughput` | `ureg.dimensionless` |

Each is 168 samples, `RegularAxis.from_rate_hz(Fraction(1, 3600))`. Four series (1, 2, 3, 5) share the **one** `delay` spec and differ only by `signal`, per the TimeNet rule. 168 = 42 × 4, so **no right-zero-padding is needed** — the collate is a no-op on length, which removes one class of silent bug.

Why each earns its slot — every series is referenced by a named clause of the rationale:

- 1 → "can you even board on time"
- 2 → "how late is your feeder likely to arrive" (the dominant term)
- 3 → "is the hub itself congested" (platform reassignment, longer walks)
- 4 → "is the connecting service being cancelled" — buffer does not help against cancellation, and this is the only channel that distinguishes `add_buffer` from `avoid_transfer`
- 5 → "the connecting train may also be late, which gives your buffer back"
- 6 → "at this hour, how often does L2 run" — the evidence for `depart_earlier` vs `add_buffer`

**Reuse cost:** series 1, 3, 4 come out of the teammate's existing `hourly` table verbatim. Only 2, 5, 6 need a new aggregate — the same SQL grouped by service group instead of `station_name`. That is roughly fifteen lines added to `extract.py`. This is the decisive argument for this design at 19 hours out.

**The service-group key.** Do not assume `line_number`. In DB/IRIS data it is well populated for S-Bahn/RE/RB and frequently empty for ICE/IC. Use a uniform key that is always populated:

```
service_group = train_type || ' → ' || final_destination_station
```

("all ICEs heading to Hamburg-Altona"). Always dense, always present, semantically honest, and it renders into a caption a human understands. Upgrade to `train_type || ' ' || line_number` only if the H0 validation gate (§5) shows ≥90 % population on the sampled corridors. Uniformity beats cleverness tonight.

## 1.4 The target: one primary scalar, two riders

**Reject (a) rank-among-K as primary.** Grounding a choice among K candidates means carrying 6×K series, and the accuracy number is then a function of how you sampled distractors — an evaluation you cannot defend in a 3-minute pitch. Its value is recoverable for free inside the prose (§1.6).

**Reject (b) door-to-door arrival as primary.** Realized arrival for an itinerary that can break is bimodal; the mean of a bimodal variable is a target the model will regress to and look bad on.

**Recommended primary — `ScalarPredictionTask`, required transfer buffer:**

```
target_name = "required_buffer_p80_min"
target      = clip(percentile_80({ d1 + m - d2 }  over horizon instances), 0, 60)
unit        = "minute"
```

where over the 7 days *following* the context window, for each scheduled instance of this connection: `d1` = observed arrival delay of the L1 feeder at X, `d2` = observed departure delay of the L2 connector at X, `m` = minimum transfer time at X.

Read plainly: *the smallest buffer that would have worked on four days out of five.* It is smooth (a percentile over ~7 instances, not one draw), decision-relevant, MAE-scoreable, has an obvious strong baseline, and it is **not recoverable from the context series by inspection** — it needs cross-line matching that the aggregates support only statistically. That gap is exactly where a temporal model should beat a text baseline.

This reframes the question from "one specific Tuesday" to *"I need to make this trip in the coming week, around 14:00 — what plan holds up?"* If a judge asks why not a single day: one draw of a heavy-tailed variable is not a plan, it is a lottery ticket. A plan is chosen against the distribution.

**Rider 1 — `ClassificationTask`, the recommended action.** Deterministic policy over the same computed numbers, fixed precedence:

| label | condition |
|---|---|
| `book_as_planned` | `required_buffer ≤ usable_slack` |
| `add_buffer` | fails, but a later L2 departure at X within 60 min holds | 
| `depart_earlier` | fails, no later connection helps, but an earlier L1 departure does |
| `avoid_transfer` | connection fails on ≥40 % of horizon instances |

where `usable_slack = scheduled_slack − m`. Four-way, majority-baseline-able, and it *is* the RECOMMENDED ACTION line of the organisers' slide.

**Rider 2 — `AnswerTask`, the prose plan.** The demo artifact. Critical construction detail: the bridge assembles the OpenTSLM `answer` as `rationale + " Answer: " + target`, so **put the STATE SUMMARY + reasoning in `rationale` and only the one-sentence action in `target`** — e.g. `target="Take the 14:07 and allow twelve minutes at Hannover Hbf."` Otherwise you get "reasoning… Answer: ‹entire paragraph›". This also satisfies the OpenTSLM prompt rule "do not name the label until the last sentence" for free.

**Optional rider 3 — `ScalarPredictionTask`, `realized_delay_travel_day_min`,** the single-draw arrival delay. Nearly free, report median AE because of the tail (the teammate's README already flags this).

Three to four tasks per record is fine as materialized tasks — `set_task_stream` is for datasets where tasks vastly outnumber records, which is not this. Say so in the write-up rather than reaching for it.

## 1.5 Ground truth without leakage

The label is computed on `[t0+168h, t0+336h)`; every series ends at `t0+168h`. Nothing crosses.

The subtler leak is **rationale-shaped**. The teammate's `rationale_for_peak` reads `horizon_delay.max()` and states it as fact. That is legitimate as a *target* (the paper shows GPT-4o the label too), but it trains the model to confidently emit numbers it cannot know, which at inference produces hallucinated precision. Rule for the route templates:

&gt; Every number in the rationale is derived from the **context window**. Horizon-derived quantities appear only in the final clause, as the answer.

**Itinerary source: derive from observed runs. Keep GTFS off the critical path.**

- gtfs.de static covers only the *next 30 days*. Your delay history is 2026-06…08. Matching a September timetable against June observations is a silent mismatch, and the gtfs.de `stops.stop_name` ↔ IRIS `station_name` join ("Berlin Hbf" vs "Berlin Hbf (tief)") is a half-day trap at 2am.
- Observed runs are self-consistent: same station naming, same period, same key space, one self-join. A candidate itinerary is a `(L1, X, L2)` triple where runs of L1 arrived at X and runs of L2 departed X with 5–45 min scheduled slack on ≥20 distinct days.

Use GTFS in exactly one narrow optional role: `transfers.min_transfer_time` for ~30 hand-checked major hubs. If that join is not clean in 30 minutes, fall back to a size-tiered constant (`m` = 5 / 8 / 10 min by hub traffic tier), documented in the card as a stated modelling assumption. Honest and bounded.

This also hands the agentic tier its best demo: an agent proposes GTFS, a deterministic validator measures the join rate, and **rejects it**. A pipeline that can reject a source is far stronger evidence of "assessment and selection" than one that accepts everything.

## 1.6 Context window ↔ departure datetime

Anchor every window so it **ends at 00:00 local on the first travel day**. Then:

- index 0 is always 00:00, index 167 always 23:00 the previous day; **index `i` has hour-of-day `i mod 24`** and the same weekday phase as the travel day minus seven. The model can learn "look at index 24k + 14 for last week's 14:00s". A strong, free inductive bias.
- The departure time enters as *text*, in the `pre_prompt` and in the annotations, never as a series offset.
- The teammate's stride-24 windows already produce exactly this alignment when the month starts at 00:00 — only the interpretation changes, no code change.

`pre_prompt` (exact shape):

&gt; You are planning a rail journey from **Frankfurt(Main)Hbf** to **Hamburg Hbf** in the week beginning **Tuesday 2026-07-14**, departing around **14:07** on **ICE → München Hbf** and changing at **Hannover Hbf** to **ICE → Hamburg-Altona**. The printed connection allows **17 minutes** at Hannover Hbf, of which **8 minutes** is the station's minimum transfer time. You are given six hourly series covering the seven days ending at midnight before travel, in this order: … Each series is normalised; its mean and standard deviation are given so you can recover real units.

`post_prompt`:

&gt; How many minutes of transfer buffer does this connection need in order to hold up on four days out of five? Reason in a single natural paragraph. Do not use bullet points, do not describe a plot, do not express uncertainty, and do not state the number until the final sentence. End with `Answer: &lt;minutes&gt;`.

## 1.7 A defect in the existing bridge that will silently ruin this dataset

`timef_bridge.py` builds the caption from `row['spec_names'][signal]`, i.e. `spec.name`. In the teammate's station dataset the three signals have three different specs so this works. In the route dataset, **series 1, 2, 3 and 5 share the one `delay` spec** — all four would be captioned `"Mean delay, it has mean … and std …:"`. Since the caption is the only channel through which identity *and* scale reach the model, four indistinguishable captions is fatal.

Fix, minimal and still domain-agnostic: the bridge prefers a record annotation `caption:&lt;signal&gt;` when present and falls back to `spec.name`. Same pattern for the pre-prompt: an optional annotation `pre_prompt_context` that the bridge uses in place of its generic sentence. Two small changes that preserve the bridge's "knows nothing about rail" selling point — which is your reusable-connector bonus item and worth protecting.

---

# 2. The agentic pipeline

Design rule throughout: **every agent emits a machine-checkable artifact, and every accept/reject decision is a deterministic predicate.** LLMs propose; code disposes.

| Tier | Stage | Kind | Emits | Bonus criterion |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | `SourceScout` | LLM + API | `candidates.jsonl` | **search** |
| 1 | `SourceScorer` | deterministic | `assessment.json` | **assessment**, **selection** |
| 2 | `SourceProbe` | deterministic | `probe.json` | **assessment**, **retrieval** |
| 3 | `SchemaMapper` | LLM, verified | `column_map.json` | **validation** |
| 4 | domain adapter | deterministic | cached parquet | **retrieval** |
| 5 | `ItineraryMiner` | deterministic | `itineraries.jsonl` | — |
| 6 | `ItinerarySelector` | LLM, quota-bound | `selected.jsonl` | **selection** |
| 7 | `RationaleWriter` | LLM, verified | `skeletons.json` | **validation** |
| 8 | `DatasetValidator` | deterministic | `validation_report.json` | **validation** |
| 9 | train / eval / rescore | deterministic | metrics | — |

**Tier 0 — generic discovery, domain-agnostic.** Input is a capability request, not a dataset name: *"hourly-or-finer operational time series, public history ≥90 days, permissive licence, no credentials, ≤100 GB."* Queries the HF datasets API (`/api/datasets?search=&amp;full=true` returns licence and size), one CKAN portal (`data.europa.eu` / `govdata.de` `/api/3/action/package_search`), and Zenodo. Normalises every hit into a `SourceCandidate {id, url, licence, size_bytes, temporal_coverage, sampling_interval, access_mode, credentials_required}`. Nothing rail-specific.

**Tier 1 — the assessment gate.** Not an LLM. Hard gates: licence in allowlist; `credentials_required == false`; interval ≤ 1h; span ≥ 90 days. Then a soft score on rows × columns × freshness. **The reject reasons are the deliverable.** Your rejection table already writes itself: Mobilithek → registration friction; RIS/Wagenreihung → partner contract; gtfs.de GTFS-RT → no history; DB Timetables/IRIS → 18h horizon, no history. Put that table on a slide.

**Tier 2 — probe.** For each survivor, actually touch it cheaply: `DESCRIBE SELECT * FROM read_parquet(url) LIMIT 0`, a `count(*)`, a 1000-row sample, a measured byte cost. This turns "instantly usable, no credentials" from a claim into a measurement. The teammate's five-column trick is precisely this stage's output.

**Tier 3 — schema mapping, the one place an LLM touches structure.** It proposes `{timestamp, entity_id, value, cancellation_flag, line_id, sequence_index} → column names`. Verified deterministically: every named column must exist in the probe schema, the timestamp must parse, the value must be numeric and in a plausible range. One re-prompt on failure, then the source is dropped. The LLM cannot survive being wrong.

**Tier 4 is the boundary.** Above it, domain-agnostic; below it, rail. A second domain needs only a new tier-4 adapter. State that boundary explicitly — it is what makes "generic discovery tier plugs in above the DB-specific adapters" a claim rather than a hope.

**Tier 6 — the curator, where LLM judgement genuinely adds value.** It picks ~200 itineraries under a *deterministic quota*: ≥25 distinct hubs, ≤8 itineraries per hub, ≥30 % of itineraries with measured connection-failure rate in [0.15, 0.5], geographic spread. Its only real freedom is realism — "Berlin→Munich via Nuremberg is a trip a person takes; two Hbf 4 km apart is not." Every pick is re-checked against the quota; violations are dropped, never negotiated.

**Tier 7 — rationale writing, and the cost trick that matters.** Do **not** paraphrase 15 000 filled rationales. Paraphrase the *skeleton with placeholders still in it*:

1. For each (action × direction) combination, ask the LLM for K = 12 paraphrased skeletons that must contain **exactly the same placeholder set** (`{ctx_l1_hour_mean}`, `{usable_slack}`, …), temp 0.3, seed 42.
2. Verify the placeholder set as a set equality. Reject and retry otherwise.
3. Fill numbers deterministically at build time, selecting a skeleton by a hash of the record id.

~100 LLM calls instead of 15 000, and **it is now structurally impossible for the LLM to invent a number** — it never sees one. That is a stronger guarantee than a post-hoc check and it fits on a slide.

Keep the post-hoc numeric-token-subset verifier anyway as a belt-and-braces check, and note the tradeoff honestly: less lexical diversity than per-record paraphrase, mitigated by K = 12 and, if time allows, per-record paraphrase on a 500-record subset.

**A consequence of the TimeNet contract that is easy to miss:** `convert()` is CPU-only, no network, so it **cannot call an LLM**. The skeletons must be generated offline at authoring time and **shipped as a committed JSON file inside the connector folder**. That is not a workaround; it is what makes the connector reproducible for anyone who runs `timenet-build`, which is the whole point of the format. Call this out.

**Tier 8 — the validator, fail closed.** Per record: exactly 6 series; each length 168; all finite; **`std &gt; 1e-6`** (a constant series z-scores to NaN and silently poisons training — this is the single most likely quiet failure); imputed fraction under cap; label in range; the `Answer:` line matching the task target exactly; no number in the rationale absent from the slot dict; split assignment consistent with the leakage rules; no record in two splits; and a **phase check** — cross-correlate series 1 and 2 at lags −1/0/+1 and assert the peak is at 0, which catches a station/line timezone desynchronisation that would otherwise be invisible and fatal. Refuse to write on any failure.

**Connector packaging.**
`datasets/error404/db-route-plan/{__init__.py, connector.py, dataset.yaml, requirements.txt, tests/}`, ending `CONNECTOR = DBRoutePlanConnector`.
- `download(cache_dir)` does **all** network work — including the remote-parquet aggregations and the itinerary self-join — and writes three local caches: `hourly_station.parquet`, `hourly_line.parquet`, `connection_instances.parquet`. Returns their paths.
- `convert(raw_refs)` reads those locally with DuckDB (local reads are not network), slices windows, computes labels, builds tasks and annotations.
- **`dataset.yaml` trap:** the card is `additionalProperties: false`. Do not add a custom `leakage:` key. Put the leakage design in `description` and as tags.
- Ship **both** datasets — `error404/db-delay-hourly` (curriculum stage 1, and the fallback) and `error404/db-route-plan` (headline) — from a shared internal module. Two registry entries is a plus, not a cost.

**Annotations per record** (a record has no metadata slot, so all of this is `Annotation(key=…, value=…)` with no span): `route_id`, `origin`, `transfer_hub`, `destination`, `line1`, `line2`, `departure_local_time`, `weekday`, `scheduled_slack_min`, `min_transfer_min`, `window_start_iso`, **`window_start_index`**, `imputed_fraction`, `n_horizon_instances`, `caption:&lt;signal&gt;` × 6, `pre_prompt_context`, **`split`**, **`split_cell`**.

`window_start_index` and `split_cell` as explicit annotations kill a real fragility: `prepare.py` currently recovers the window index with `record.record_id.rsplit("-", 1)[1]`, which will break the moment record ids carry route structure.

---

# 3. Leakage control for a route-level target

## What breaks in the teammate's design

1. **Station holdout does not transfer.** A route touches three stations. Require all three unseen and with 20 % of stations held out you get P ≈ 0.008 — an empty cell. Require any one unseen and you contaminate roughly half the corpus. Both degenerate.
2. **Line leakage is new, and worse.** One service group appears across dozens of itineraries. A model that memorises "ICE → Hamburg-Altona runs 12 min late" transfers everywhere. Station holdout does nothing about it.
3. **Corridor near-duplicates.** A→X→B and A→X→B′ share five of six series. Random splitting puts near-copies on both sides of the wall.
4. **The embargo is now under-specified.** With a 168h horizon (up from 24h), the autocorrelation being broken spans a week, so `EMBARGO_HOURS = CONTEXT_HOURS` should become `CONTEXT_HOURS + HORIZON_HOURS = 336`. This is a judgement call, not a bug — the train/test windows already do not overlap in the existing code — but state the reasoning.

## The fix

Split on **transfer-hub identity**, not stations, plus a line axis.

- **Axis T (time):** early → train, late → test, 336h embargo. Unchanged in spirit.
- **Axis R (route):** hold out **whole transfer hubs** — every 5th hub by traffic rank is never a transfer hub in training. The hub owns series 3 and 4 *and* the label is computed there, so this is the meaningful test: *can you reason about a connection at a hub you have never seen?*
- **Axis L (line), reported as a diagnostic not a cell:** every 7th service group never appears as L1 or L2 in training. Report `line_unseen` as a fifth number so the 2×2 table stays readable.

**Be honest about the weakening.** A held-out hub may still appear as a *boarding* station in some training route. "Never appears anywhere" is unachievable at route level — the network is connected. State that plainly in the card and the pitch rather than implying a cleaner holdout than you have. Judges reward the disclosure; they punish discovering it themselves.

**Near-duplicate control** (cheap, usually forgotten): cap itineraries at ≤ 8 per transfer hub, and forbid two records sharing `(transfer_hub, line_pair, window_start)`. Without this you ship "15 000 records" that are really "twelve routes seen a thousand times", and every metric is meaningless.

**Compute the split inside `convert()` and store it as the `split` annotation.** The leakage design then ships *with the dataset* rather than living in a downstream script — which is the right architecture and also a strong point to make about the format. Keep the `assign_split` callable in the bridge for ad-hoc re-splitting.

---

# 4. Riskiest assumptions, and the fallback ladder

| # | Risk | Probability | Mitigation |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Service-group key is dirty or sparse | **high** | Uniform `(train_type, final_destination_station)`; H0 gate decides |
| 2 | Too few well-supported itineraries | medium | Only **A and X** need density, **B is unconstrained** — this widens the pool a lot; also mine at top-120 stations, not top-40 (extraction costs seconds) |
| 3 | **Labels degenerate** — most connections just work, `book_as_planned` ≫ 70 %, `required_buffer` = 0 | **high** | Stratified curator quota (§2 tier 6); 5-minute SQL check at H3.5; if the whole network's connections hold, pivot the primary scalar to realized door-to-door delay, which is never degenerate |
| 4 | Station/line series desynchronise by an hour (tz handling differs between the two aggregates) | medium, **invisible** | Stay in 2026-06…08 (no DST fold, as the teammate's README already notes); validator cross-correlation phase check |
| 5 | OpenTSLM collate with N = 6 | low | Fixed N avoids variable-length lists; verify on CPU at H6.5 *before* any GPU spend |
| 6 | Nebius `injectedFiles` ≤ 64 KiB | medium | Skeleton JSON + built dataset go to the S3 bucket mounted as a volume; injectedFiles carries only a bootstrap script. This bites at 3am if unplanned |
| 7 | **Neither `timenet` nor `opentslm` is importable in this working copy** — I verified | certain | Someone must confirm a reproducible env with pinned `timenet[build]`, `timenet-connectors`, `opentslm`, `duckdb` before H2. The connector folder needs its own `requirements.txt` regardless |
| 8 | Generated prose contradicts its own answer | medium | Extend the teammate's existing contradiction test to the route rationale |
| 9 | TimeNet "archival raw" vs. our imputation | low, but a format judge will notice | Imputed hours are baked in (OpenTSLM needs finite values); store `imputed_fraction` and the imputed-hour count per record, and name the tension in the card description |

## Fallback ladder — each rung is a shippable submission

- **F0 (exists now):** the per-station dataset plus a trained model. Ships regardless of everything below.
- **F1 (H8):** route dataset built and validated, **raw templates as rationales, no LLM paraphrase**, trained on the scalar + classification tasks. A complete submission.
- **F2 (H13):** AnswerTask prose from paraphrased skeletons; caption-only ablation.
- **F3 (H16, stretch):** the binary itinerary-choice task; GTFS `min_transfer_time` join.

**Hard abort rule, written down now so nobody has to invent it at 4am:** if at **H6** the miner has produced fewer than 60 itineraries with ≥20 supported days, drop the transfer framing and fall back to the **single-leg** variant — four series `{board_station_delay, line_delay, line_service, dest_station_delay}`, target = p80 realized arrival delay at B for that line at that hour, action ∈ {go as planned / take the earlier train / allow N min}. It still answers "a route from A to B", still uses only dense aggregates, and reuses ~90 % of the code.

## Baselines — non-negotiable, because "the connector earns its place" is the whole claim

1. **Text baseline** — same numbers serialised into the prompt, no connector. Free via `QADataset._format_sample_str`.
2. **Persistence / climatology** — `clip(ctx_l1_hour_mean + m − usable_slack, 0, 60)`. The honest bar.
3. **Majority class** for the action task.
4. **Caption-only ablation** — captions (which carry mean and std!) with **zeroed series**. This detects whether the model reads the series at all or only the summary statistics in the caption. Almost nobody runs it; it costs one flag in the bridge and it is a credibility multiplier in a three-minute pitch.

One more gap worth closing, flagged in your own `ideas.md`: the rubric leads with "a useful problem" and this direction has no named user. Name two — the **traveller with a connection to make**, and the stronger B2B one, the **timetable planner who wants to know which advertised connections are fiction.** The dataset measures the second one directly.

---

# 5. Hour-by-hour, 19 hours (H0 = now, submit at H19)

Two-person split named where it matters; the H0.5–H7 stretch is more than one person's work.

| Window | Work |
|---|---|
| **H0–H0.5** | **Validation gate.** Three queries: `line_number` population by `train_type`; distinct `(train_type, final_destination_station)`; mined connection candidates at top-20 hubs with ≥20 supported days. Go/no-go on the service-group key and on transfer-vs-single-leg framing. |
| **H0.5–H2** | **A:** `lines.py` (hourly service-group aggregate) + `itineraries.py` (the self-join → `itineraries.jsonl` with support stats). **B:** freeze `dataset.yaml` and scaffold the `error404/db-route-plan` connector folder. |
| **H2–H3.5** | **A:** `labels.py` — p80 required buffer, action class, realized delay, plus the full context-derived slot dict. **B:** starts TimeF assembly against the synthetic-DuckDB fixture (reuse the teammate's `test_db_delay.py` pattern) — this breaks the dependency on A. |
| **H3.5–H4** | **Label-distribution check.** Action histogram + buffer quantiles. Degenerate → stratified quota or the risk-3 pivot. **Decision point.** |
| **H4–H5.5** | `route_timef.py` — 6 series, 3 specs, all annotations, 3 tasks/record, raw-template rationales. Structurally a clone of `timef.py`. |
| **H5.5–H6.5** | Leakage splitter + tier-8 validator, fail-closed. Hub holdout, line holdout, 336h embargo, near-duplicate cap. |
| **H6.5–H7** | **Bridge smoke test on CPU:** `rows_from_timef` → N=6 through the collate → one forward pass with `google/gemma-3-270m`. Includes the caption fix (§1.7). Do this *before* spending a cent of GPU. |
| **H7–H8** | **CHECKPOINT F1.** First Nebius L40S run, scalar + classification, raw templates. ~$3–5. |
| **H8–H9.5** | *(while F1 runs)* Rationale skeletons: 12 per action × direction, placeholder-set verifier, committed as JSON in the connector folder. |
| **H9.5–H10.5** | AnswerTask prose assembly (STATE SUMMARY / RECOMMENDED ACTION / EXPECTED OUTCOME), rebuild, revalidate. |
| **H10.5–H11** | Read F1 metrics; extend `rescore.py` for the new task names and cells. |
| **H11–H13** | **Main run.** All tasks, curriculum stage 1 (station) → stage 2 (route), LoRA on gemma-3-270m, new stage registered in `curriculum_learning.py`. ~$8. |
| **H13–H14** | All four baselines, including the caption-only ablation. |
| **H14–H15** | **Agentic tiers 0–3 made real.** It will be tempting to skip; do not — it is a scored bonus. Time-box: HF API + one CKAN portal + the deterministic scorer + the probe. The **rejection table** is the artifact. It need not sit upstream of the build at runtime; it must be runnable and must have *produced* the recorded decision. |
| **H15–H16** | `timenet-build --quiet build` both datasets, validate both cards, run connector tests, tag versions. |
| **H16–H17.5** | Demo + pitch: one route, one departure, six plots, the generated plan, the metric table with four leakage cells and four baselines, the rejection table. |
| **H17.5–H18.5** | Buffer / second GPU run if metrics disappoint. |
| **H18.5–H19** | Submit. |

Total GPU spend ≈ $15 against a $1000 voucher. Compute is not the constraint; the itinerary miner and the label distribution are.
