# §3. Span-bearing annotations in practice

## 3.1 `TimeInterval.seconds` — exact signature

`/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-gaurav-Downloads-zurich-lates-temporal-ai-error404-ai/6f11fbbe-b41b-4ac6-96cf-af296c485bf7/scratchpad/TimeNet/packages/timenet/src/timenet/types/spans.py`

```python
    @classmethod
    def seconds(cls, start: float, end: float, *, time_series_ids: tuple[str, ...] | None = None) -&gt; "TimeInterval":
        """Construct an interval from recording seconds, each bound rounded to the nearest microsecond.

        Args:
            start: Start of the interval, in recording seconds.
            end: End of the interval, exclusive, in recording seconds.
            time_series_ids: Series the interval is scoped to. ``None`` covers every series.

        Returns:
            The interval.
        """
        return cls(start_us=seconds_to_us(start), end_us=seconds_to_us(end), time_series_ids=time_series_ids)
```

- `start` and `end` are **positional-or-keyword**; `time_series_ids` is **keyword-only** (after the `*`).
- `time_series_ids` **is optional**, defaults to `None`.
- `None` means **whole record** — every series in the record. Confirmed by the class docstring on `TimeSpan`: *"Covers the whole record when `time_series_ids` is `None`, or a subset of series when it names them."*
- `()` (empty tuple) is **rejected**: `"TimeSpan time_series_ids must be None (whole record) or non-empty, got ()"`.
- The companion `TimeInterval.micros(start, end, *, time_series_ids=None)` takes whole microseconds — **use this for DB**, because `seconds()` goes through float and `

... [23524 characters truncated] ...

pe_id(qt)) for qt in sorted(question_types)]
        for template_id in sorted(template_ids):
            annotations.append(Annotation(key="template_id", value=template_id, id=_template_ann_id(template_id)))
            options = answers.get(template_id)
            if options:
                annotations.append(Annotation(key="answer_options", value=list(options), id=_options_id(template_id)))
        annotations.extend(
            Annotation(key="clinical_context", value=context, id=_context_id(context)) for context in sorted(contexts)
        )
```
Note `value=list(options)` — a list value is legal; `value_type_of` tags it `"list"` and the manifest stores it as a JSON array. `Annotation.__post_init__` canonicalizes a tuple to a list so the in-memory object equals its read-back form.

**Legal `value` types** (`value_type_of`): `bool`, `int`, `float`, `str`, `list`/`tuple`→`list`, `dict`→`map`. Anything else raises `unsupported annotation value type`. `None` means "pure marker" and then a `span` is mandatory:
```python
        if self.value is None and self.span is None:
            raise TimeFValidationError(
                f"annotation {self.key!r} has neither a value nor a span, so it says nothing. Give it "
                f"a value, or a span to mark a region of the timeline"
            )
```

## 4.3 `Annotation.unit`

```python
    unit: str | pint.Unit | None = None
    """Optional physical unit of ``value``. Give a unit string (for example ``"years"``) or a
    :class:`pint.Unit`. Construction validates the unit against the shared registry. An unrecognized
    string raises ``ValueError``. Construction stores a ``pint.Unit`` as its canonical name."""
```

- **It accepts both.** A `pint.Unit` is stored as `str(unit)`; a string is kept **as written** but validated.
- Validation goes through `normalize_unit` (`/…/packages/timenet/src/timenet/types/units.py`):
  ```python
  if unit is None:
      return None
  if isinstance(unit, pint.Unit):
      return str(unit)
  try:
      ureg.Unit(unit)
  except (pint.UndefinedUnitError, pint.errors.DefinitionSyntaxError, ValueError) as exc:
      raise TimeFValidationError(
          f"unknown unit {unit!r}; pass a pint unit (e.g. ureg.millivolt) or a unit string pint "
          f"recognizes, or omit unit="
      ) from exc
  return unit
  ```
  (The docstring says `ValueError`; `TimeFValidationError` subclasses it.)
- The registry is **private to TimeNet** and defines two extras: `beat` and `bpm`. Everything else is stock pint.
- **When to use it:** whenever `value` is a physical quantity. Real usages: `unit="years"` (age, twice). That's the entire production set. `ScalarPredictionTask.unit` uses the identical `normalize_unit` path (`unit="bpm"`, `unit="year"`).
- **For DB:** `Annotation(key="delay", value=12.0, unit="minute")` works (`minute` is stock pint). `"min"` also resolves. `"minutes"` resolves. Note `unit="year"` vs `unit="years"` both validate and are stored **differently** — pick one spelling and be consistent, because `derive_schema` raises on conflicting descriptors for the same key.

## 4.4 `Annotation.description` and `Annotation.source`

```python
    description: str | None = None
    """Optional human-readable description of the annotation."""
    source: str | None = None
    """Optional per-instance provenance (the rater, method, or model behind this value). Unlike
    ``description``, which the descriptor holds once per key, ``source`` can differ between
    annotations that share a key."""
```

The distinction is structural, not stylistic:
- **`description` is type-level.** It is projected into `AnnotationDescriptor` (one per *key*) and lands in the manifest schema. **Every annotation sharing a key must carry the same description**, or `derive_schema` raises `annotation 'k' has conflicting descriptors across records`. `sleep_edfx` encodes this as a rule: *"What each key of a closed set states. Every value of one key states the same thing, so the description belongs to the key and not to the annotation built for a value."*
- **`source` is instance-level.** It is *not* in `AnnotationDescriptor`, so it varies freely per annotation.

**Real usage:** `description` is used heavily by `sleep_edfx` (the `_DESCRIPTIONS` table, `build_age`, `build_lights_off`, `build_recording_start_local`, `build_demographics_note`, `build_vocabularies`). **`source` has zero usages anywhere in the repo** — no connector, no test, no doc example. It exists but is unexercised.

**For DB:** put "Delay in minutes at the reference station, from the DB Timetables API `changed_time` minus `planned_time`" in `description` (once per key). Put `"gtfs-rt"` vs `"iris-scrape"` vs `"manual"` in `source` per-instance.

## 4.5 `subject_ids` — what it's FOR, and what actually consumes it

**Declaration** (`/…/packages/timenet/src/timenet/dataset/record.py:210-211`):
```python
    subject_ids: tuple[str, ...] = ()
    """Subjects this record belongs to (empty for subject-less domains)."""
```

**`add_record` signature** (`/…/dataset.py:53-61`): `subject_ids: tuple[str, ...] = ()`, keyword-only, docstring: *"The subjects that this record belongs to. The tuple is empty for domains that have no subjects."*

**Exhaustive downstream trace** — every reference in `packages/*/src`:

| File:line | What it does |
|---|---|
| `/…/timenet/writer/writer.py:248` | `values["subject_id"].extend(record.subject_ids)` — feeds `_resolve_id_types`, which picks `binary(16)` vs `string` storage per logical id |
| `/…/timenet/writer/writer.py:524` | `"subject_ids": codec.encode_list("subject_id", record.subject_ids)` — writes the column |
| `/…/timenet/format/schemas.py:61,130` | Declares `("subject_ids", pa.list_(id_types["subject_id"]))` in the records Arrow schema |
| `/…/timenet/reader/reader.py:778` | Decodes it back onto the `Record` |
| `/…/timenet/testing/builders.py:211` | `assert exp.subject_ids == act.subject_ids` in `assert_datasets_equal` |

**That is the complete list. There is nothing else.**

- **Splits:** no split machinery exists in the repo at all. No group-k-fold, no subject-aware splitter. `search` (client/registry) filters on domain, task, license, spec, tag, id, query — never subject.
- **Torch loader:** `/…/packages/timenet/src/timenet/torch.py` `__getitem__` returns `{"record_id", "series", "series_masks", "tasks", "annotations"}`. **`subject_ids` is not in the item dict.**
- **Dedupe:** the writer dedupes series by `time_series_id` and annotations by `id`. `subject_id` participates only in the id-type resolution (all-canonical-UUID → 16-byte storage), which is a *storage* optimization, not a semantic dedupe.

**How it differs from an annotation:** it is a first-class *column* on the record (a list of ids), not a key/value pair. It has no `value`, no `unit`, no `description`, no `span`, and it does **not** reach `derive_schema` / the manifest annotation schema. It's an identity join key, meant to be read by a downstream consumer writing their own subject-aware split.

**Recommendation for your station/line/hub identities:**

| Identity | Where | Why |
|---|---|---|
| The **one entity the record is about** (e.g. the station whose delay series this is, `"DE:08000105"`) | `subject_ids=("DE:08000105",)` | This is the leakage boundary you'd group a split on. It is exactly the "subject" role. Costs nothing, and a consumer can do `GroupShuffleSplit` on it. |
| Line/operator/hub-class, product category, region | span-less `Annotation` | They are *facts about* the record, need `value`/`description`, and want to show up in the manifest schema so `describe()` and search surface them. Use the shared-instance cache (`MetadataAnnotation` pattern) since they come from closed sets. |
| Human-readable station name, DS100 code | span-less `Annotation` on the record | Same as above; keep the machine id in `subject_ids` and the label in an annotation. |
| A journey/trip that touches several stations | `subject_ids=(station_a, station_b, ...)` is legal (it's a tuple) | But nothing downstream reads it, so prefer one primary id + annotations unless you're writing your own splitter. |

Because nothing downstream reads `subject_ids`, **anything you put only there is invisible to `describe()`, to `search`, and to the torch loader.** If you want it queryable, it must also be an annotation.

## 4.6 `DatasetSchema` / `AnnotationDescriptor` — is there a declaration step?

**No. Annotation keys are inferred, never declared.** There is no place to register a key up front.

`/…/packages/timenet/src/timenet/types/metadata.py:238-250`:
```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class DatasetSchema:
    """A dataset's type declaration, derived from its data (never hand-authored).

    It holds flat descriptor instances for specs and annotations. It also holds the real built-in
    :class:`~timenet.types.tasks.Task` subclasses, resolved against the registry instead of reconstructed.
    """

    time_series_specs: tuple[TimeSeriesSpec, ...] = ()
    """Descriptors for the dataset's measurement modalities."""
    annotations: tuple[AnnotationDescriptor, ...] = ()
    """Type-level descriptors for the dataset's annotation keys."""
    tasks: tuple[type[Task], ...] = field(default=())
    """Built-in ``Task`` subclasses the dataset declares, resolved from the registry."""
```

`/…/packages/timenet/src/timenet/types/annotations.py`:
```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class AnnotationDescriptor:
    """Type-level projection of an annotation key, stored in the schema and manifest."""

    key: str
    annotation_type: AnnotationType
    value_type: str | None = None
    unit: str | None = None
    description: str | None = None

    def __post_init__(self) -&gt; None:
        """Validate ``unit`` against the shared registry."""
        object.__setattr__(self, "unit", normalize_unit(self.unit))
```

### `derive_schema` — verbatim

`/…/packages/timenet/src/timenet/dataset/dataset.py:416-471`:

```python
    def derive_schema(self) -&gt; DatasetSchema:
        """Walk the dataset's instances and build its :class:`DatasetSchema`.

        This method collects the distinct spec, annotation, and task types. It stores the result
        on the dataset, and it returns the result.

        Returns:
            The derived :class:`DatasetSchema`.

        Raises:
            TimeFValidationError: If one spec type or annotation key yields conflicting descriptors
                across records.
        """
        specs = self._ordered_unique(ts.spec for record in self._records for ts in record.time_series)
        by_spec_type: dict[str, object] = {}
        for spec in specs:
            existing = by_spec_type.get(spec.spec_type)
            if existing is not None:
                raise TimeFValidationError(
                    f"spec_type {spec.spec_type!r} has conflicting TimeSeriesSpec contracts: {existing!r} and {spec!r}"
                )
            by_spec_type[spec.spec_type] = spec
        record_annotations = (annotation for record in self._records for annotation in record.annotations)
        annotations = self._ordered_unique(
            AnnotationDescriptor(
                key=annotation.key,
                annotation_type=annotation_type_of(annotation),
                value_type=value_type_of(annotation.value),
                # The __post_init__ method normalizes unit to a plain string or None, so this
                # value is always str | None.
                unit=cast("str | None", annotation.unit),
                description=annotation.description,
            )
            # Registered (task-referenced) annotations carry descriptors too, so their key/type reach
            # the schema even though no record carries them.
            for annotation in (*record_annotations, *self._registered_annotations.values())
        )
        by_key: dict[str, AnnotationDescriptor] = {}
        for descriptor in annotations:
            existing = by_key.get(descriptor.key)
            if existing is not None:
                raise TimeFValidationError(
                    f"annotation {descriptor.key!r} has conflicting descriptors across records: "
                    f"{existing!r} and {descriptor!r}"
                )
            by_key[descriptor.key] = descriptor
        # A streamed dataset declares its task types up front, so the schema records them without
        # draining the stream just to collect them.
        streamed = self._ordered_unique(self._streamed_task_types)
        tasks = streamed if self._task_stream is not None else self._ordered_unique(type(task) for task in self._tasks)
        self._schema = DatasetSchema(
            time_series_specs=tuple(specs),
            annotations=tuple(annotations),
            tasks=tuple(tasks),
        )
        return self._schema
```

**The trap you must design around:** the descriptor is `(key, annotation_type, value_type, unit, description)`. Two annotations with the **same key** but any differing component of that tuple **abort the whole build**. Concretely:

- `Annotation(key="delay", value=3)` and `Annotation(key="delay", value=3.0)` → `value_type` `int` vs `float` → **raises**. **Cast every numeric annotation value to a single Python type at the connector boundary.**
- `Annotation(key="cause", value="construction")` (static) and `Annotation(key="cause", value="construction", span=...)` (interval) → different `annotation_type` → **raises**. **A key is one shape across the whole dataset.** Your planned-disruption interval annotation needs a distinct key from any static disruption flag.
- Same key with `description=None` on one record and a description on another → **raises**. Set descriptions from a module-level table keyed by annotation key, exactly as `sleep_edfx._DESCRIPTIONS` does.
- Same key with `unit="minute"` on one and `unit="min"` on another → **raises**.

`derive_schema` is called by the engine (`engine.py:69`) before `store_dataset`, so this fires on every real build. Call it in your own test (`_convert().derive_schema()`) the way `hello_world` does.

# §5. Tasks

## 5.1 Full constructor signatures

All task dataclasses are `@dataclass(kw_only=True)` — **every argument is keyword-only, and every field has a default**, including on the base. Source: `/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-gaurav-Downloads-zurich-lates-temporal-ai-error404-ai/6f11fbbe-b41b-4ac6-96cf-af296c485bf7/scratchpad/TimeNet/packages/timenet/src/timenet/types/tasks.py`.

### Shared base fields (inherited by all three)

| Field | Type | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | `str` | `field(default_factory=new_id)` → UUIDv7 | Pass explicitly for a deterministic build |
| `record_ids` | `tuple[str, ...]` | `()` | **`add_task` sets this for you.** Set it manually only for streamed tasks |
| `prompt` | `str \| None` | `None` | |
| `scope` | `Span \| None` | `None` | `None` = whole record |
| `input_annotation_ids` | `tuple[str, ...]` | `()` | |
| `target` | overridden per subclass | `None` | |
| `target_annotation_ids` | `tuple[str, ...]` | `()` | Mutually exclusive with `target` |
| `rationale` | `str \| None` | `None` | Chain of thought, on **every** task type |
| `from_tasks` | `tuple["Task", ...]` | `()` | Instances, not ids; `from_task_ids` is a derived property |

Class-vars (not constructor args): `task_type`, `refs`, `answer_fields = ("target",)`, `answer_is_record = False`, `target_is_scalar`.

### `ClassificationTask`
```python
@dataclass(kw_only=True)
class ClassificationTask(Task):
    task_type: ClassVar[TaskType] = TaskType.CLASSIFICATION
    target_is_scalar: ClassVar[bool] = True
    target: str | None = None
    """The label."""
    target_schema: str | None = None
    """Name of the label vocabulary the target belongs to."""
```
Constructor: base fields + `target: str | None = None`, `target_schema: str | None = None`. Nothing required at construction; `add_task` requires exactly one of `target` / `target_annotation_ids`.

### `AnswerTask`
```python
@dataclass(kw_only=True)
class AnswerTask(Task):
    task_type: ClassVar[TaskType] = TaskType.ANSWER
    target_is_scalar: ClassVar[bool] = True
    target: str | None = None
    """The answer, or the caption when the task is unprompted."""
```
Constructor: base fields + `target: str | None = None`. **No extra fields at all.** A caption is an `AnswerTask` with no `prompt`; a CoT QA is one with `rationale` set.

### `ScalarPredictionTask`
```python
@dataclass(kw_only=True)
class ScalarPredictionTask(Task):
    task_type: ClassVar[TaskType] = TaskType.SCALAR_PREDICTION
    target_is_scalar: ClassVar[bool] = True
    target: float | None = None
    """The predicted value."""
    unit: str | pint.Unit | None = None
    """Optional physical unit of ``target``. Give a unit string (for example, ``"bpm"``) or a
    :class:`pint.Unit`. The constructor checks it against the shared registry. It stores a
    ``pint.Unit`` as its name."""
    target_name: str | None = None
    """Name of the quantity to predict (for example, ``"mean_heart_rate"``)."""

    def __post_init__(self) -&gt; None:
        """Normalize ``unit`` against the shared registry so it is always a plain string or ``None``."""
        self.unit = normalize_unit(self.unit)
```
Constructor: base + `target: float | None = None`, `unit: str | pint.Unit | None = None`, `target_name: str | None = None`. Both extras optional. `unit` validated at construction.

**This is your delay-minutes task.** `ScalarPredictionTask(target=7.0, unit="minute", target_name="arrival_delay")`.

## 5.2 `target_schema` — what format?

**A plain `str | None`. Not pipe-separated, not a list.** `format/schemas.py:261` stores it as `pa.string()`, and `writer/encodings.py:59` dictionary-encodes it (`_TASK_CATEGORICAL`). Its own docstring says only *"Name of the label vocabulary the target belongs to."*

Real usages, in two distinct styles:

**Style A — a bare name (docs, benchmarks, unit tests):**
```python
# docs/data-model/tasks.md, docs/types.md
ClassificationTask(target="faulty", target_schema="condition")
# benchmarks/end_to_end/corpus.py
target_schema="condition"   /   target_schema="rhythm"   /   target_schema="sleep-stage"   /   target_schema="mean-sign"
# packages/timenet/tests/types/test_tasks.py:46
whole = ClassificationTask(target="afib", target_schema="rhythm")
# packages/timenet/tests/reader/test_reader.py:1013
dataset.add_task(record, ClassificationTask(target="afib", target_schema="scp5", id="cls-0"))
```

**Style B — the *id of a registered annotation that holds the set* (the only production connector).** `/…/sleep_edfx/tasks.py` module docstring:
```
A ``target_schema`` is a different thing. It must equal the id of the registered annotation that
holds the set, so ``name_vocabulary`` builds both from one string.
```
Implementation:
```python
def name_vocabulary(id_prefix: str, key: AnnotationKey) -&gt; str:
    """Give the id of the annotation that holds one closed set.

    Returns:
        The id, which a task also states as its ``target_schema``.
    """
    return f"{id_prefix}-vocabulary-{key}"


def build_vocabularies(id_prefix: str) -&gt; list[Annotation]:
    """Give one annotation for each closed set a task draws its target from.

    These belong to no record, so the caller registers them rather than attaching them.

    A set is one annotation and not one for each member. One annotation for each member states
    that the values exist without stating that they are the whole set.
    """
    return [
        Annotation(
            key=f"{key}_vocabulary",
            value=list(labels),
            description=f"The values a {key!r} target takes, as the release writes them.",
            id=name_vocabulary(id_prefix, key),
        )
        for key, labels in _VOCABULARIES
    ]
```
Usage: `target_schema=name_vocabulary(id_prefix, AnnotationKey.SEX)` → `"sleep-edfx-vocabulary-sex"`, and `dataset.register_annotations(tasks.build_vocabularies(_ID_PREFIX))` publishes the matching annotation. Its README states why:
&gt; The connector registers the label vocabularies, and does not attach them. ... `target_schema` on a task names the set that its target draws from. A name alone tells a consumer nothing about what is in the set.

**Nothing in the framework validates or resolves `target_schema`.** It is an opaque string. Style B is strictly better and is what I'd copy: `target_schema="db-vocabulary-delay_cause"` plus a registered `Annotation(key="delay_cause_vocabulary", value=[...], id="db-vocabulary-delay_cause")`.

`test_mean` shows the null case: `assert all(task.target_schema is None for task in ...)` — omit it entirely for an ad-hoc binary label.

## 5.3 `input_annotation_ids` — intended use and downstream consumption

```python
    input_annotation_ids: tuple[str, ...] = ()
    """Annotations given to the model as context, not ones it must produce."""
```

**Intended use:** hand the model grounding context by reference, instead of duplicating it into every task row. `hello_world`'s comment states it plainly:
```python
                    # The cohort annotation gives context to the model. The model does not need to
                    # produce this annotation.
                    input_annotation_ids=(cohort.id,),
```

**Production usage** — `ecg_qa_cot`, ~230k tasks:
```python
                input_ids = [_qtype_id(row["question_type"]), _template_ann_id(template_id)]
                if answers.get(template_id):
                    input_ids.append(_options_id(template_id))
                input_ids.append(_context_id(_clinical_context(row)))
                yield AnswerTask(
                    prompt=str(row["question"]),
                    target=str(row["answer"]),
                    rationale=str(row["rationale"]),
                    input_annotation_ids=tuple(input_ids),
                    ...
                )
```
This is the whole point of `register_annotations`: four metadata values per question, stored once each rather than 230k times.

**What consumes it downstream — the complete list:**

| Consumer | File:line | Behavior |
|---|---|---|
| Validation | `/…/dataset/dataset.py:549` (`_check_annotation_refs`) | Raises if an id resolves to neither a target record's annotation nor a registered one |
| Writer | `/…/writer/writer.py:826` | `codec.encode_list("annotation_id", task.input_annotation_ids)` |
| Format | `/…/format/schemas.py:239,253` | `("input_annotation_ids", pa.list_(id_types["annotation_id"]))` |
| Reader | `/…/reader/reader.py:604` | Decodes back onto the task |
| **Copy-on-write editor** | `/…/dataset/edit.py:179,220` | **The only semantic consumer.** When a subset is taken, it *drops* unreachable input ids rather than failing: `input_annotation_ids=tuple(aid for aid in task.input_annotation_ids if aid in reachable)` — with the comment *"`input_annotation_ids` is context given to the model, not the answer."* Contrast: an unreachable `target_annotation_ids` invalidates the task. |
| Torch loader | — | **Does not read it.** The item dict has `tasks` and `annotations` separately; resolving the reference is the user's job. |

So: it round-trips faithfully, it is validated, the editor treats it as droppable context — but **no training code in this repo dereferences it.** It's a data contract for downstream consumers.

## 5.4 Multiple tasks on one record; can they share a `rationale`?

**Yes to multi-task records; no shared-rationale mechanism.** `rationale: str | None` is a plain per-instance field on the base. There is no interning, no reference-by-id. Two tasks wanting the same rationale each set the string; the parquet writer dictionary-encodes task columns (`writer/encodings.py`) so duplicate strings cost little on disk, but in memory they're separate.

**Shipped connectors with &gt;1 task per record:**

**`timenet/hello_world`, record-0 — 4 tasks in one batch:**
```python
        classification = ClassificationTask(target="normal", id="task-cls-0")
        dataset.add_tasks(
            record0,
            [
                classification,
                AnswerTask(prompt="What rhythm?", target="Normal.", rationale="...", input_annotation_ids=(cohort.id,), from_tasks=(classification,), id="task-answer-0"),
                ScalarPredictionTask(target=60.0, unit="bpm", target_name="mean_rate", id="task-scalar-0"),
                TemporalLocalizationTask(prompt="...", mode=LocalizationMode.SPARSE, target_annotation_ids=(stimulus.id, artifact.id), id="task-localize-0"),
            ],
        )
```
Only the `AnswerTask` carries a rationale.

**`physionet/sleep_edfx` — ~2450 tasks per record, three types, streamed.** `/…/sleep_edfx/tasks.py`:
```python
def iter_tasks(records: Sequence[Record], id_prefix: str) -&gt; Iterator[Task]:
    for record in records:
        span_annotations = [one for one in record.annotations if one.span is not None]
        non_span_annotations = [one for one in record.annotations if one.span is None]
        stages = [one for one in span_annotations if one.key == AnnotationKey.SLEEP_STAGE]

        yield from build_epoch_tasks(record.record_id, id_prefix, stages)
        yield from build_record_tasks(record.record_id, id_prefix, non_span_annotations, stages)
        moment = build_lights_off_task(record.record_id, record.time_span, span_annotations)
        if moment is not None:
            yield moment
```
`build_record_tasks` yields, per record: one `ScalarPredictionTask` (age), one `ClassificationTask` (sex), optionally one more (condition), optionally one `TemporalLocalizationTask` (the night). `build_epoch_tasks` yields one `ClassificationTask` per 30 s epoch. **No rationales anywhere** — the release states no reasoning.

**The multi-task conventions, as practiced:**
1. **One `add_tasks` batch per record** when the count is small; a **task stream** when it isn't.
2. Streamed tasks **set their own `record_ids`** and are validated per-task without cross-task state (`_validate_streamed_task`). They do **not** appear in `Record.task_ids`. From the sleep_edfx README: *"Streamed tasks are trusted and not validated, so each task carries its own `record_ids`, and none appears in `Record.task_ids`."* You cannot mix `add_task` with `set_task_stream` — `_register_batch` raises *"add_task/add_tasks cannot be used on a streamed dataset"*.
3. **Ids:** either name every task deterministically (`f"task-{index}"`, `f"ecgqa-{split}-{index}"`) or let all of them auto-generate. sleep_edfx explicitly chose the latter: *"No task names its own id. Nothing resolves one by id... Every task takes the generated id."* Deterministic ids are what make `assert_datasets_equal` and a byte-stable rebuild possible — **name them.**
4. **Derive the task set from annotations already on the record**, never from a second read of the source: *"A second read of a hypnogram can disagree with the annotations the records carry."*

**Streaming API** for reference (`dataset.py:209`): `set_task_stream(task_types: Sequence[type[Task]], source: Callable[[], Iterator[Task]])` — the source must be **re-iterable** (a lambda returning a fresh generator), because the writer walks it more than once.

## 5.5 `from_tasks` — real usage and purpose

```python
    from_tasks: tuple["Task", ...] = ()
    """Source tasks this one was derived from."""

    @property
    def from_task_ids(self) -&gt; tuple[str, ...]:
        return tuple(task.id for task in self.from_tasks)
```

**It holds Task *instances*, not ids.** Two real usages in the entire repo, and they are the same code:

`/…/datasets/timenet/hello_world/connector.py:182`:
```python
        classification = ClassificationTask(target="normal", id="task-cls-0")
        dataset.add_tasks(record0, [
            classification,
            AnswerTask(..., from_tasks=(classification,), id="task-answer-0"),
            ...
        ])
```
`/…/packages/timenet/src/timenet/testing/builders.py:152` — the same construction, mirrored into the test-fixture builder.

Plus its assertion at `/…/hello_world/tests/test_connector.py:71`:
```python
def test_has_a_task_chain():
    dataset = _convert()
    assert any(task.from_tasks for task in dataset.tasks), "expected a task derived via from_tasks"
```

**What it's for:** provenance/composition — recording that a free-text answer was derived from a prior categorical label, so a consumer can train the chain or evaluate the steps separately. `docs/data-model/tasks.md`: *"Tasks can also build on each other. With `from_tasks`, one task feeds into another. So a simple label can [ground a richer question]."*

**What it enforces** (`_validate_task_batch`, `_check_no_derivation_cycle`):
- A parent must be already-registered **or in the same batch** — otherwise `derives from task X, which is not registered in this dataset or part of the batch`.
- Self-reference raises: `lists itself in from_tasks`.
- Cycles within a batch raise: `tasks in the batch form a cyclic from_tasks derivation` (detected by Kahn's algorithm — batch order is irrelevant).
- The reader relinks `from_tasks` to real instances on load (`docs/timef-reader.md:106`).

**For DB:** if you emit both a "was this train delayed &gt;6 min?" classification and a "why?" answer per record, chain the second off the first. It costs one extra field and it's the only way the format records that they're the same question at two levels.

---

# §6. Reproducibility contract

## 6.1 What state may `download()` rely on?

**Contract** (`/…/packages/timenet/src/timenet/connectors/base.py:69-83`):
```python
    def download(self, cache_dir: Path) -&gt; list[TRaw]:
        """Fetch or discover raw source files and return lightweight references to them.

        I/O only: no parsing, no array work. Must be idempotent for a given ``cache_dir``. Override
        this for a synchronous connector. For an I/O-bound one, override :meth:`download_async`
        instead and leave this default, which drives it to completion, since the engine calls
        connectors synchronously.
        """
        return asyncio.run(self.download_async(cache_dir))
```

Constraints, all enforced or stated:
- **No constructor arguments.** `BaseConnector.__init__` takes only `self` and its only job is: *"A subclass that overrides neither would instantiate and only fail deep in the engine. Catch it at construction instead."*
- **Idempotent for a given `cache_dir`.** The engine creates `&lt;TIMENET_CACHE&gt;/&lt;dataset_id&gt;/` and **deletes it after a successful build** unless `--keep-cache` or a caller-supplied `cache_dir` (`engine.py:74-76`: *"Only clean a cache that we created. A caller-supplied `cache_dir` is user-owned."*).
- Implement **exactly one** of `download` / `download_async`.

### The env-var / optional-credentials question

**`docs/connectors.md` line ~57 says:** *"A connector takes no constructor arguments. Configuration comes from environment variables that `__init__` reads."*

**No connector in the tree does this.** A repo-wide grep for `os.environ` / `getenv` across `packages/*/src` returns exactly two hits, neither in a connector:
```
packages/timenet-connectors/src/timenet_connectors/builder/env.py:145:    child_env = {**os.environ, "TIMENET_ISOLATION": "off"}
packages/timenet/src/timenet/registry/s3.py:249:  endpoint = os.environ.get("AWS_ENDPOINT_URL_S3") or os.environ.get("AWS_ENDPOINT_URL")
```

**The actual documented pattern for optional credentials: delegate to the client library.** Two shipped examples:

**HuggingFace** — `/…/bases/huggingface.py` module docstring:
&gt; The base reads `HF_TOKEN` from the environment, so gated datasets work with no extra wiring. Fully private datasets have no auto-parquet ref, and this base does not support them.

TimeNet code never touches `HF_TOKEN`; `huggingface_hub` reads it. Absent token → public datasets still build; gated ones fail at the Hub call.

**S3/PhysioNet** — `/…/ecg_qa_cot/connector.py:39-41`:
```python
# PTB-XL 500 Hz records from PhysioNet's open S3 bucket. ``_hr`` means high-rate (500 Hz) recordings.
# Fetched with boto3, which resolves AWS credentials itself (anonymous access works for this open bucket).
PTBXL_ZIP_URL = "s3://physionet-open/ptb-xl/ptb-xl-1.0.3.zip"
```

**Is there a shipped connector that degrades gracefully — present = richer, absent = still builds?** **No.** Every shipped connector either needs no credential at all or fails hard. There is no `if token: ... else: ...` branch anywhere. The formal mechanism for gated data is not a runtime branch but a **card declaration** (`/…/types/metadata.py`):
```python
    access: Access = Access.OPEN
    """How a user obtains the data (see :class:`Access`). ``OPEN`` needs nothing."""
    access_url: str | None = None
    """Where to obtain access (the DUA or credentialing page). Required when ``access`` is not ``OPEN``."""
```
with `__post_init__` enforcing `access_url is required when access is &lt;non-open&gt;`, and `docs/connectors.md`:
&gt; A credentialed dataset (a PhysioNet DUA-gated one, for example) declares `access: credentialed` and an `access_url` on its card. TimeNet never hosts such data, so it is build-your-own: get access at the `access_url`, set the provider credential in the environment (the way `HF_TOKEN` already works), and run `timenet-build build &lt;id&gt;` yourself. A `load` or `download` of a credentialed dataset from a hosted registry raises with the `access_url` instead of serving bytes.

**For your DB API key:** you're inventing the pattern, not following one. The idiomatic-for-this-repo choice is a helper in your connector module, not `__init__`:

```python
def _client_credentials() -&gt; tuple[str, str] | None:
    """Return the DB API credentials, or None for the open subset."""
    client_id = os.environ.get("DB_CLIENT_ID")
    api_key = os.environ.get("DB_API_KEY")
    return (client_id, api_key) if client_id and api_key else None
```
called from `download`, not `__init__`. Read it in `download` so a `convert`-only unit test never needs it. And note: a degrading connector produces **different datasets from the same `dataset_version`**, which breaks the version contract below. If credentials change the output, bump `dataset_version` or split into two dataset ids.

Also note `.env` is honored: `SettingsConfigDict(env_prefix="TIMENET_", env_file=".env", extra="ignore")` — but only for `TIMENET_*` keys.

## 6.2 Is the build deterministic? What hashes what?

**Short answer: the format hashes its *outputs*, never its *inputs*. Determinism is entirely the connector author's responsibility, and it is unverified by the framework.**

**What exists:**
- **Output checksums.** `/…/packages/timenet/src/timenet/format/checksums.py`: `sha256` over every data artifact, recorded per-file in `manifest.files` as `"sha256:&lt;hex&gt;"` and verified by `TimeFReader.verify()`. This detects corruption in transit; it does **not** compare two independent builds.
- **Idempotence by version, not by content.** `run_pipeline` (`engine.py:56-59`):
  ```python
  version_dir = root / metadata.dataset_id / str(metadata.dataset_version)
  committed = (version_dir / MANIFEST_FILE).exists()
  if committed and not force:
      return version_dir
  ```
  A rebuild is skipped if `&lt;dataset_version&gt;/manifest.json` exists. **The version string in `dataset.yaml` is the identity — nothing checks that the same version means the same bytes.**

**What does NOT exist:**
- No hash of the downloaded sources. No expected-checksum field on the card. No content-addressed version.
- No `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` / mtime normalization.
- No cross-build byte comparison in CI.

**Two things actively break byte-stability if you don't handle them:**
1. **Auto ids are UUIDv7 → timestamped and random.** `/…/types/ids.py`:
   ```python
   unix_ms = time.time_ns() // 1_000_000
   value = (unix_ms &amp; 0xFFFFFFFFFFFF) &lt;&lt; 80  # 48-bit timestamp in the high bits
   ...
   value |= secrets.randbits(12) &lt;&lt; 64  # rand_a
   ```
   Every `record_id`, `time_series_id`, `annotation_id`, `task_id` left to default differs between builds. `add_record`'s docstring: *"Pass an explicit ID for deterministic output, for example for golden test fixtures."* **Name every id from source data.** sleep_edfx: *"Named and not generated, thus two builds of one archive give one set of ids."*
2. **`build_env` embeds the full package set** (below), so the manifest differs across machines even when the data is identical.

**The connector-side determinism idioms that are actually used:**
- Fixed seed: `_SEED = 20260715  # fixed base seed keeps every build byte-for-byte identical` (`test_mean`).
- Deterministic ids from source keys: `f"ptbxl-{ecg_id}"`, `f"ecg-{ecg_id}-{name}"`, `f"{_ID_PREFIX}-{recording.recording_id}"`, `_context_id()` = `sha1(context)[:12]`.
- Sorted iteration over sets before emitting: `for ecg_id, split in sorted(split_of_ecg.items())`, `sorted(question_types)`, `sorted(template_ids)`, `sorted(contexts)`. **Never iterate a `set` unsorted in `convert`.**
- The test: `assert_datasets_equal(_convert(), _convert())` in **all four** connector test modules.

**For DB, the hard part:** the delay API is a live feed. Two builds a day apart return different data under the same `dataset_version`. There is no framework support for this. Your options are (a) pin a snapshot date in `dataset_version` / the card description and cache the raw pull, or (b) accept that `1.0.0` means "the connector", not "the bytes". `sleep_edfx` avoids the problem entirely by pulling a frozen archive.

## 6.3 What exactly goes into `build_env`

`/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-gaurav-Downloads-zurich-lates-temporal-ai-error404-ai/6f11fbbe-b41b-4ac6-96cf-af296c485bf7/scratchpad/TimeNet/packages/timenet/src/timenet/provenance.py` — the whole file:

```python
"""Describe the environment a build ran in, for the manifest's provenance block."""

from importlib.metadata import distributions
import platform
from typing import Any


def build_env() -&gt; dict[str, Any]:
    """Describe this process's Python version and installed packages.

    Under an isolated build the recorded set is exactly the base, the connector's requirements, and
    their transitives. Under ``--no-isolation`` it is the whole ambient environment, which is noisier
    but still the truth about what produced the dataset.

    Returns:
        A dict with ``python`` (the interpreter version) and ``packages`` (name -&gt; version, sorted).
    """
    packages: dict[str, str] = {}
    for dist in distributions():
        name = dist.metadata["Name"]
        if name:
            packages[name] = dist.version
    return {"python": platform.python_version(), "packages": dict(sorted(packages.items()))}
```

So exactly two keys:
- `python`: `platform.python_version()`, e.g. `"3.13.2"`.
- `packages`: `{distribution name: version}` for **every** installed distribution, sorted by name.

**Not** included: git SHA, hostname, OS, timestamp, source URLs, source checksums, CLI flags, env vars.

Called once, at `/…/packages/timenet/src/timenet/writer/writer.py:678`: `build_env=build_env()`. Stored on the `Manifest` (`manifest/manifest.py:61`) with this docstring:
```python
    build_env: dict[str, Any] = field(default_factory=dict)
    """The Python version and package set that produced this version.

    Provenance only: nothing reads it to interpret the data. It is here so a builder can answer what
    produced a dataset version without re-deriving it from a build log.
    """
```
`docs/manifest.md:56-58` repeats: *"Like `value_encoding`, `build_env` is provenance only. No code reads it to interpret the data."*

**The practical implication:** under isolation, `packages` is a tight, reproducible closure (base + your `requirements.txt` + transitives). Under `--no-isolation` it's your whole dev venv. **This is a reason to always ship a `requirements.txt`** even if it only pins one library — it makes the recorded environment meaningful.

## 6.4 `requirements.txt` + `TIMENET_ISOLATION` — how the isolated env works

### The flag

Tri-state, resolved in `/…/builder/cli.py:120-123`:
```python
    # The flag is tri-state. An explicit --isolation wins over TIMENET_ISOLATION=off. An isolated
    override = None if isolation is None else ("on" if isolation else "off")
    if settings(isolation=override).isolation == "on":
```
Precedence: `--isolation`/`--no-isolation` flag → `TIMENET_ISOLATION` env var → default `"on"` (`config.py:27`).

### The mechanism

`/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-gaurav-Downloads-zurich-lates-temporal-ai-error404-ai/6f11fbbe-b41b-4ac6-96cf-af296c485bf7/scratchpad/TimeNet/packages/timenet-connectors/src/timenet_connectors/builder/env.py`. Module docstring:

```python
"""Run a connector's build in an environment built from its own requirements.

The parent resolves the connector's ``requirements.txt`` without importing the connector (see
:func:`timenet_connectors.discovery.requirements_for`). It layers that over a base pinned to the
parent's own ``timenet`` and ``timenet-connectors``, then runs ``timenet-build build`` in that
environment. uv owns resolution and caching. A repeat build with an unchanged requirement set is a
cache hit.
"""
```

Step by step:

1. **Locate the requirements without importing the connector.** `discovery.requirements_for(dataset_id)`:
   ```python
   path = connector_dir(dataset_id) / "requirements.txt"
   return path if path.is_file() else None
   ```
   and `connector_dir` is *deliberately import-free*:
   &gt; This runs before the connector's environment exists, so importing the connector module (which is what `resolve` does) would fail on the very dependencies its `requirements.txt` declares. `find_spec` imports the parent packages only, never the leaf.
   
   It also uses the presence of `dataset.yaml` to distinguish a connector dir from an org namespace dir.

2. **Pin the base to the parent's own distributions** (`_base_args`):
   ```python
   BASE_DISTRIBUTIONS = ("timenet", "timenet-connectors")
   """Pinned to the parent's own versions, so the code that writes the TimeF bytes is the code that
   reads them back. ``timenet-connectors`` depends on ``timenet[build]``, so the card-YAML
   dependencies arrive transitively and the extra never needs naming here."""
   ```
   Editable/local-path installs become `--with-editable &lt;src dir&gt;` (via `direct_url.json`); index installs become `--with name==version`.

3. **Step outside any virtualenv** (`_base_interpreter`):
   &gt; uv reads a virtualenv passed as `--python` as the *base* of the ephemeral environment and layers the `--with` overlay on top of it. Every parent site-package stays importable in the child, and a package the parent already has silently satisfies a connector requirement instead of being resolved. The venv's own base interpreter carries none of that.
   
   ```python
   return getattr(sys, "_base_executable", None) or sys.executable
   ```

4. **Build the uv command** (`uv_command`):
   ```python
   command = [uv, "run", "--no-project", "--python", spec.python, *spec.base]
   if spec.requirements is not None:
       command += ["--with-requirements", str(spec.requirements)]
   return [*command, *argv]
   ```
   with:
   &gt; Both halves of the isolation are load-bearing. `--no-project` stops uv from discovering and syncing whatever project the parent runs in. `spec.python` points outside any virtualenv, so uv has nothing to layer the environment over.

5. **Re-exec the same CLI as a child, with the recursion guard:**
   ```python
   argv = ["timenet-build", *(["--quiet"] if quiet else []), "build", dataset_id, "--out", str(out)]
   ...
   command = uv_command(env_spec(dataset_id, values_backend=values_backend), argv)
   # TIMENET_ISOLATION=off is the recursion guard: the child is this same CLI.
   child_env = {**os.environ, "TIMENET_ISOLATION": "off"}
   ```
   **The child inherits the parent's full `os.environ`**, so your `DB_API_KEY` reaches it.
   
   stderr is tee'd live (so downloads show progress) and captured; stdout is captured whole because its last line is the version directory.

6. Zarr special case: `if values_backend != "parquet": base += ("--with", f"timenet[zarr]=={...}")` — the parent can't import the connector to learn its declared backend, so it over-provisions.

### What fails if a dep is missing

**Three distinct failure modes:**

**(a) You forgot `requirements.txt` entirely, isolation on.** The base env has only `timenet` + `timenet-connectors` + transitives. Your lazy import raises `ImportError` inside the child; the parent re-raises with the child's tail:
```python
        raise TimeNetBuildError(
            f"building {dataset_id!r} failed with exit code {returncode}. Command: {shlex.join(command)}.{detail}"
        )
```

**(b) You listed it but imported eagerly, running `--no-isolation`.** The module-level import fails at discovery time. `discovery._connector_spec` deliberately does **not** mask this:
```python
        # find_spec imports the parent packages. An unknown id fails to find one of *our* modules. A
        # broken import inside an existing connector fails to find something else. Surface that real
        # error instead of masking it as "unknown connector".
        if exc.name and (module_name + ".").startswith(exc.name + "."):
            return None  # a missing parent package (for example, an unknown org)
        raise
```
**This is why the lazy import is mandatory, not stylistic.**

**(c) The guarded case — the pattern to copy.** `/…/bases/huggingface.py`:
```python
        try:
            from huggingface_hub import hf_hub_download, list_repo_files  # noqa: PLC0415
        except ImportError as exc:
            raise ImportError(
                f"reading {self.HF_REPO!r} needs huggingface_hub, declared in this connector's "
                "requirements.txt. Run the build without --no-isolation, or install it yourself"
            ) from exc
```
Same shape in `/…/bases/physionet.py:88` (wfdb) and `/…/download/s3.py:47` (boto3). Note the `# noqa: PLC0415` — ruff flags non-top-level imports and the repo suppresses it here by convention.

**Missing uv:**
```python
        raise TimeNetBuildError(
            "cannot run an isolated build: no uv executable found. Install uv, or build in this "
            "interpreter with TIMENET_ISOLATION=off."
        ) from exc
```

### `requirements.txt` conventions

Shipped examples — **note the comment-per-dependency style, which is universal:**

`/…/chengsenwang/tsqa/requirements.txt`:
```
# huggingface_hub downloads the source dataset from the HuggingFace Hub.
huggingface_hub&gt;=0.24
```

`/…/physionet/sleep_edfx/requirements.txt`:
```
# edfio parses the EDF container: the header, the per-signal ranges and the data
# records. Pure Python, with numpy its only dependency.
edfio&gt;=0.4.14
# boto3 gets the archive from the physionet-open S3 bucket.
boto3&gt;=1.34
# xlrd reads the two subject tables. They are legacy BIFF8 workbooks, which
# openpyxl cannot open at all.
xlrd&gt;=2.0
```

Rules from `docs/connectors.md`:
&gt; List every dependency the connector needs, even one that another connector already names. There are no shared requirement fragments. If several connectors share one file, an edit to that file can break a connector that you did not check.

And from `SKILL.md` step 7:
&gt; If you added or changed a `requirements.txt`, re-run `make sync` so the new library lands in your own environment. Otherwise `ty` reports your lazy import as unresolved and the connector test can't run.

`AGENTS.md` confirms `make test-connectors` runs each connector's tests **in an environment built from its own requirements**.

---

# Distilled checklist for your DB connector

**Layout** — `/…/packages/timenet-connectors/src/timenet_connectors/datasets/db/rail_delays/`:
`__init__.py`, `connector.py` (ends `CONNECTOR = ...`), `dataset.yaml` (`dataset_id: db/rail-delays`), `requirements.txt` (comment each dep, import lazily with a guarded `ImportError`), `tests/__init__.py`, `tests/test_connector.py` (inline fixture; `_convert()` helper; `test_is_a_connector`, `test_metadata`, `assert_datasets_equal(_convert(), _convert())`, plus a solvability assertion, plus `_convert().derive_schema()`). Add `datasets/db/__init__.py` for the new org.

**Axis** — `RegularAxis.from_rate_hz(Fraction(1, 3600))` for hourly. This forces the **time frame**; every span is a `TimeInterval`/`TimePoint` in microseconds. Prefer `.micros()` over `.seconds()` at these magnitudes.

**Construction windows** — `Annotation(key="planned_disruption", value=..., span=TimeInterval.micros(a, b))` with **`time_series_ids=None`**, and set `Record.time_span = TimeInterval.micros(0, max(series_end, disruption_end))` so the overrun validates cleanly instead of warning. Do not clip; if you must, keep the true bounds in `value`/`description`. A zero-length window is illegal — use a `TimePoint`.

**Identities** — primary station id in `subject_ids` (inert but correct, and it's what a consumer would group a split on); line/operator/hub-class as span-less annotations built through a `MetadataAnnotation`-style shared-instance cache.

**Schema traps** — one shape and one value type per annotation key, forever; descriptions from a module-level `_DESCRIPTIONS` table keyed by annotation key; one unit spelling per key. `derive_schema` aborts the build otherwise.

**Tasks** — `ScalarPredictionTask(target=..., unit="minute", target_name="arrival_delay")` for delay minutes; `ClassificationTask(target=..., target_schema="db-vocabulary-delay_cause")` plus a matching `register_annotations([...])` vocabulary; chain a "why" `AnswerTask` off the classification with `from_tasks=`.

**Determinism** — name every record/series/annotation/task id from source keys; sort every set before iterating; no auto-UUIDs anywhere.</result>

# 1. Ground truth from OpenTSLM's own results

## 1a. Dataset statistics (paper Table 4, `arXiv:2510.02410v3` §A.1)

Verbatim from Table 4:

| Stage | Dataset | #Samples (Train/Val/Test) | Num series | Length | Freq | Derived points/series |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | TSQA | 38,400 / 4,800 / 4,800 | 1 | "Hours to Years" | n/s | — |
| 1 | M4-Captions | 80,000 / 10,000 / 10,000 | 1 | 64–512 points | n/s | 64–512 |
| 2 | HAR-CoT | 68,542 / 8,718 / 8,222 | 3 | 2.56 s | 50 Hz | **128** |
| 2 | Sleep-CoT | **7,434 / 930 / 930** | 1 | 30 s | 100 Hz | **3000** (paper) / **1500** (code) |
| 2 | ECG-QA-CoT | **159,313 / 31,137 / 41,093** | 12 | 10 s | 100 Hz | **1000** |

Two discrepancies worth knowing, both verifiable:

- **Sleep length.** `src/opentslm/time_series_datasets/sleep/sleepedf_cot_loader.py:75` asserts `len(parsed[0]) == 1500` — i.e. 30 s at **50 Hz**, not the 100 Hz the paper claims. The shipped data is 1,500 points.
- **ECG train count.** Table 4 says 159,313; Table 7 and the repo README (`src/opentslm/time_series_datasets/ecg_qa/README.md`) both say **159,306**. Use 159,306.

## 1b. Full results (paper Table 2) — macro-F1 / Accuracy

**Table 3 — Flamingo with a frozen pretrained Chronos-2 encoder instead of the from-scratch one.** This is the single most important ablation in the paper for your budget:

| Encoder | TSQA F1 | HAR-CoT F1 | Sleep-CoT F1 | ECG-QA F1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Llama3.2-1B, scratch | 94.08 | 62.93 | 49.33 | 34.62 |
| **Llama3.2-1B + Chronos-2** | **99.71** | **66.82** | **64.57** | **41.92** |
| Llama3.2-3B, scratch | 90.14 | 62.77 | 45.45 | 40.25 |
