| Gemma3-1B-pt, scratch | 92.56 | 65.44 | 43.69 | 35.31 |
| Gemma3-1B-pt + Chronos-2 | 99.71 | 62.72 | 61.13 | 40.25 |

**+15.24 F1 on Sleep-CoT and +7.30 on ECG-QA from swapping the encoder, at identical dataset size.** No amount of extra data in this paper buys that.

## 1c. Why Sleep-CoT (7,434) beats ECG-QA-CoT (159,306) — four causes, in order of size

**The paper never explains this.** It contains no sentence relating dataset size to performance. I searched: the only "scaling" discussion in the whole paper (line 1038, §A.7.2) is **memory** scaling. So the following is my reconstruction from the code and the dataset definitions — but every piece is checkable.

**Cause 1 (largest): the two datasets do not use the same split protocol, despite the paper claiming they do.**

The paper asserts, twice: *"All datasets are split into 80/10/10 train/validation/test sets"* (§4.1) and *"All data was split into ratios of 80/10/10 (train/val/test)"* (§A.1). Check the arithmetic:

- Sleep-CoT: 7,434 + 930 + 930 = 9,294 → **80.0 / 10.0 / 10.0**. ✅
- ECG-QA-CoT: 159,306 + 31,137 + 41,093 = 231,536 → **68.8 / 13.4 / 17.7**. ❌

68.8/13.4/17.7 is exactly the ECG-QA paper's **patient-disjoint 7.2 : 0.8 : 2.0** split ([ECG-QA, NeurIPS 2023 D&amp;B](https://arxiv.org/html/2306.15681): *"split into training and test sets according to an 8:2 ratio based on their patient IDs before generating QA samples to prevent the overlapping of ECGs"*). The repo confirms it inherits pre-made split CSVs verbatim (`src/opentslm/time_series_datasets/ecg_qa/ecgqa_cot_loader.py:155-166`, reading `ecg_qa_cot_{train,val,test}.csv`), and the repo README reproduces 159,306/31,137/41,093 as ECG-QA's own filtered counts.

Sleep-CoT, by contrast, is split in-repo with a **random stratified split over individual 30-second epochs**:

```python
# src/opentslm/time_series_datasets/sleep/sleepedf_cot_loader.py:85-96
train_val_df, test_df = train_test_split(
    df, test_size=TEST_FRAC, random_state=seed, stratify=df['label'])
train_df, val_df = train_test_split(
    train_val_df, test_size=val_frac_adj, random_state=seed+1, stratify=train_val_df['label'])
```

No subject column, no night column, no `GroupShuffleSplit`. Sleep-EDF's 9,294 epochs come from on the order of tens of nights. **A 30 s epoch at t=3600 s and its neighbour at t=3630 s from the same night land on opposite sides of the split with probability ~0.2.** They are near-duplicates: same subject, same electrode impedance, same drift, usually the same stage. This is textbook subject-level leakage.

The magnitude of this effect is well quantified in the HAR literature. [Dehghani, Sarbishei, Glatard &amp; Shihab, *Sensors* 19(22):5026, 2019](https://doi.org/10.3390/s19225026) found overlapping-window improvements of 8–13% F1 under subject-dependent CV that **vanish or reverse (−1 to −4%) under leave-one-subject-out**, concluding: *"the performance improvements regarding overlapping windowing reported in the literature seem to be associated with the underlying limitations of subject-dependent cross validation."*

So: **Sleep-CoT 69.88 vs ECG-QA-CoT 32.84 is partly an apples-to-oranges comparison between a leaky split and an honest one.** You cannot read a data-efficiency conclusion off it.

**Cause 2: supervision density per decision, not sample count.**

| Dataset | Train samples | Distinct decisions | **Samples per decision** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sleep-CoT | 7,434 | 5 classes (one question) | **1,487** |
| HAR-CoT | 68,542 | 8 classes (one question) | **8,568** |
| ECG-QA-CoT | 159,306 | **3,138 unique questions**, 42 templates, 103-answer vocabulary | **50.8** (36.8 per template×answer cell) |

The paper states this itself: *"we excluded comparison questions, retaining 42/70 templates. This yielded 3,138 unique questions across 240k samples"* (§4.1), and *"OpenTSLM achieves 40.25 F1 on ECG-QA-CoT, producing rationales across 3,138 questions and 42 templates with diverse answer options"* (§5). The 103-answer vocabulary is from the ECG-QA source paper (88 attributes + 12 leads + yes/no/not-sure).

**ECG-QA-CoT has 21× more rows than Sleep-CoT and 29× less supervision per decision.** This is the whole story of the marginal value of a sample: a sample is only worth something to the extent that it is the *n*-th example of a decision the model must make. Rows spread across 3,138 questions are 3,138 tiny datasets, not one big one.

**Cause 3: normalise by chance, and the ranking changes completely.**

| Dataset | SP/Llama-1B F1 | Random F1 | **Lift** | Train samples |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| HAR-CoT | 65.44 | 11.49 | **5.70×** | 68,542 |
| Sleep-CoT | 69.88 | 17.48 | **4.00×** | 7,434 |
| TSQA | 97.50 | 33.33 | 2.93× | 38,400 |
| ECG-QA-CoT | 32.84 | 16.47 | **1.99×** | 159,306 |

Rank-correlation between training-set size and lift over chance across the four datasets is **negative**. That is the single most quotable fact in this whole report.

**Cause 4: a train/eval objective mismatch that hits ECG-QA hardest.**

All three CoT rationale sets were generated by GPT-4o given a **two-way** choice. Appendix A.2 quotes the prompts:

- HAR (§A.2.1): *"This data corresponds to one of two possible activities: [CORRECT_ACTIVITY] [DISSIMILAR_ACTIVITY]"* — with a hand-written *dissimilarity* map (sitting is paired against walking/running/biking, never standing).
- Sleep (§A.2.2): *"This signal corresponds to one of two possible sleep stages"* — again with a dissimilarity map (N2 is paired only against W and REM, never N1 or N3).
- ECG (§A.2.3): *"This question has one of two possible answers"*.

So the rationale supervision teaches a **binary discrimination between deliberately dissimilar classes**, while evaluation is 5-, 8-, or 103-way. For Sleep the residual 5-way task is still nearly solvable because Wake-vs-N3 is the hard axis and the dissimilarity map covers it. For ECG-QA the 2-way→103-way gap is enormous. The paper concedes the mechanism obliquely in Limitations: *"we generated CoT datasets using GPT-4o on plots, which we have shown to perform poorly on these plots alone. Curated datasets likely lead to better rationales"* and *"framing tasks as natural language generation does not ensure that the model prioritizes the correct label after presenting its rationale."*

**What this implies for marginal value of samples:** in this architecture, going from 7,434 → 159,306 samples bought **nothing**, because the extra samples were spent widening the decision space rather than deepening any decision. Your operative planning quantity is **samples per (question × class) cell**, and OpenTSLM's own evidence brackets it: ~50/cell → 2× chance; ~1,500/cell → 4× chance; ~8,500/cell → 5.7× chance. **Most of the return is captured by ~1,000–1,500 samples per decision cell.**

---

# 2. Explicit sample-efficiency evidence

## 2a. Is there a data-scaling ablation? **No.**

I grepped the full v3 HTML (including the entire appendix, 3,303 lines) for `scaling|ablat|learning curve|sample.?effic|fraction of|subset of the training|data.?efficien|fewer samples|low.?resource`. Hits: 8 lines, **all** about VRAM/memory scaling (§4.4, §A.7.2, Fig. 17/18, Tables 10/11) or generic prose. There is **no** learning curve, no N% -of-data experiment, no seed variance, no error bars anywhere in Tables 2 or 3. Every reported number is a single run.

**This matters for your jury:** the paper does not license any claim of the form "OpenTSLM needs N samples." The only near-controlled comparison in the paper is the encoder swap (Table 3), and it says architecture beats data.

## 2b. Early stopping: 200 epochs / patience 5 — and the code disagrees with the paper

The paper, §A.1.1: *"Training Length: Up to 200 epochs with early stopping (patience = 5 epochs)"*.

The repo does not do that.

- `src/opentslm/model_config.py:13-14`: `NUM_EPOCHS = 20`, `EARLY_STOP_PAT = 5`
- Per-stage overrides in `curriculum_learning.py`: stage1 `num_epochs=30` (L1246), stage2 `=20` (L1271), stage3 `=30` (L1296), stage4 `=60` (L1322), stage5 `=60` (L1348)
- And the **docstrings contradict the code they sit on**: stage1 docstring says "Epochs: 20" (code 30), stage2 "Epochs: 15" (code 20), stage3 "Epochs: 100" (code 30). These are the fossils of tuning-down passes.

Early-stopping mechanics, `curriculum_learning.py:1162-1188`: improvement threshold `avg_val_loss + 1e-4 &lt; best_val_loss`; counter `epochs_no_improve`; break at `&gt;= EARLY_STOP_PAT`.

**Where did it actually stop?** Nowhere in the repo. The trainer writes `loss_history.txt` per stage (`_save_loss_history`, referenced at L1159) into `results/{llm_id}/{model_type}/`, but `results/` is **not committed** — `find` over the repo returns zero `results*` directories and zero `.jsonl`/loss fixtures. The only committed data artifacts are `evaluation/clinicianecg/pipeline/config.json` and two markdown files. **The paper reports no epoch counts either.** So this signal is unavailable; do not let anyone claim otherwise.

**The one thing the epoch caps do tell you** is what the authors believed after running it: they gave the 7,434-sample Sleep stage **60** epochs and the 68,542-sample HAR stage **30**. Roughly constant sample-presentations, i.e. they budgeted by *steps*, not epochs — 60 × 7,434 ≈ 446k presentations vs 30 × 68,542 ≈ 2.06M. Not constant, but the small dataset got 2× the epochs.

## 2c. Optimizer steps per stage (batch_size = 4, `model_config.py:10`)

`total_steps = num_epochs * len(train_loader)` (`curriculum_learning.py:1045`), warmup 3% (`WARMUP_FRAC`) in code vs "10%" in the paper — another code/paper divergence.

| Stage | Train N | Steps/epoch (÷4) | Max steps (code epochs) | Max steps (paper's 200) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 TSQA | 38,400 | 9,600 | 288,000 | 1,920,000 |
| 2 M4 | 80,000 | 20,000 | 400,000 | 4,000,000 |
| 3 HAR-CoT | 68,542 | 17,136 | 514,080 | 3,427,200 |
| 4 Sleep-CoT | 7,434 | 1,859 | **111,540** | 371,800 |
| 5 ECG-QA-CoT | 159,306 | 39,827 | 2,389,620 | 7,965,800 |

**Your budget of "a few hundred to a few thousand steps" is 1.5–3 orders of magnitude below every OpenTSLM stage.** Even the smallest stage, Sleep-CoT, is 1,859 steps for a *single* epoch. This is the most important number in the report and it drives §6.

## 2d. Stage-1 warm-up: how much does the encoder need before stage 2 works?

The paper: *"In stage one (encoder warmup), we use two synthetic time-series datasets to pretrain the encoder"* — TSQA (38,400) + M4-Captions (80,000) = **118,400 samples, ~688,000 optimizer steps of cap**, before any CoT task is touched.

Critically, from `curriculum_learning.py:1547-1563`:

```python
def _enable_lora_if_needed(self, stage_name: str):
    if self.model_type != "OpenTSLMSP": return
    stages_with_lora = ["stage3_cot", "stage4_sleep_cot", "stage5_ecg_cot"]
    if stage_name in stages_with_lora:
        model.enable_lora(lora_r=16, lora_alpha=32, lora_dropout=0.0)
```

**LoRA is off for stages 1 and 2.** Stages 1–2 train only encoder + projector (2,375,424 params, see §3). The 118,400 warm-up samples exist purely to get the from-scratch patch encoder out of random initialisation before the LLM is allowed to adapt to it.

There is **no ablation of how much warm-up is enough**. But you have two escape hatches, and you should take one:

1. **Skip it.** The authors publish stage checkpoints on HF Hub under `OpenTSLM/{base}-{dataset}-{type}` — e.g. `OpenTSLM/llama-3.2-1b-tsqa-sp`, `OpenTSLM/llama-3.2-1b-m4-sp` (README lines 89, 168–181; demo scripts `demo/huggingface/01_test_hf_tsqa.py`, `02_test_hf_m4.py`). Start from the M4 checkpoint and your encoder is already warm.
2. **Delete the problem.** Use Chronos-2 frozen, as in Table 3: +15.24 F1 on Sleep-CoT for zero extra data and zero warm-up steps.

---

# 3. Trainable parameter count

Llama-3.2-1B config: `hidden_size` 2048, `num_hidden_layers` 16, `num_attention_heads` 32, `num_key_value_heads` 8 (head_dim 64 → **kv projection width 512**), `intermediate_size` 8192.

Source: `src/opentslm/model/encoder/TransformerCNNEncoder.py`, `src/opentslm/model/projector/MLPProjector.py`, `src/opentslm/model/llm/OpenTSLMSP.py:100-119`, `src/opentslm/model_config.py`.

### TransformerCNNEncoder (defaults: `output_dim=128`, `num_heads=8`, `num_layers=6`, `patch_size=4`, `ff_dim=1024`, `max_patches=1024`)

| Component | Arithmetic | Params |
|---|---|---|
| `patch_embed = Conv1d(1, 128, k=4, s=4, bias=False)` | 128 × 1 × 4 | 512 |
| `pos_embed = Parameter(1, 1024, 128)` | 1 × 1024 × 128 | **131,072** |
| `input_norm = LayerNorm(128)` | 2 × 128 | 256 |
| **Per `TransformerEncoderLayer`** (d=128, nhead=8, ff=1024): | | |
|  · `self_attn.in_proj_weight` | 3 × 128 × 128 | 49,152 |
|  · `self_attn.in_proj_bias` | 3 × 128 | 384 |
|  · `self_attn.out_proj` | 128×128 + 128 | 16,512 |
|  · `linear1` (128→1024) | 1024×128 + 1024 | 132,096 |
|  · `linear2` (1024→128) | 128×1024 + 128 | 131,200 |
|  · `norm1` + `norm2` | 2 × 256 | 512 |
|  · **subtotal / layer** | | **329,856** |
| × 6 layers (`nn.TransformerEncoder` deep-copies; **no** weight sharing, `norm=None`) | 329,856 × 6 | **1,979,136** |
| **Encoder total** | 512 + 131,072 + 256 + 1,979,136 | **2,110,976** |

### MLPProjector (`LayerNorm(128)` → `Linear(128, 2048)` → GELU → Dropout)

| Component | Arithmetic | Params |
|---|---|---|
| `LayerNorm(128)` | 2 × 128 | 256 |
| `Linear(128, 2048)` | 128 × 2048 + 2048 | 264,192 |
| **Projector total** | | **264,448** |

### LoRA r=16, α=32, dropout 0.0, bias="none", on 7 projections × 16 layers

Per adapter: `r·d_in + d_out·r`.

| Module | d_in → d_out | Arithmetic | Params |
|---|---|---|---|
| `q_proj` | 2048 → 2048 | 16·2048 + 2048·16 | 65,536 |
| `k_proj` | 2048 → **512** | 16·2048 + 512·16 | 40,960 |
| `v_proj` | 2048 → **512** | 16·2048 + 512·16 | 40,960 |
| `o_proj` | 2048 → 2048 | 16·2048 + 2048·16 | 65,536 |
| `gate_proj` | 2048 → 8192 | 16·2048 + 8192·16 | 163,840 |
| `up_proj` | 2048 → 8192 | 16·2048 + 8192·16 | 163,840 |
| `down_proj` | 8192 → 2048 | 16·8192 + 2048·16 | 163,840 |
| **per layer** | | | **704,512** |
| **× 16 layers** | | | **11,272,192** |

### Total

| | Params | Share |
|---|---|---|
| TransformerCNNEncoder | 2,110,976 | 15.5% |
| MLPProjector | 264,448 | 1.9% |
| LoRA (16 layers × 7 modules) | 11,272,192 | 82.6% |
| **TOTAL TRAINABLE** | **13,647,616** | **≈ 1.10% of the 1.236 B backbone** |
| *(stages 1–2 only, LoRA off)* | *2,375,424* | *0.19%* |

## 3b. Rules of thumb, and precisely where they break

**These are heuristics, not theorems. State that first, then use them.**

| Heuristic | Applied here | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| **10 samples per trainable parameter** (classical, from linear/logistic models and the "events per variable" literature) | 10 × 13,647,616 = **136 M samples** | **Absurd, and disproven by the paper itself.** Sleep-CoT trained on 7,434 samples = **5.4 × 10⁻⁴ samples/param** and got the paper's best number. Off by 5 orders of magnitude. |
| **1 sample per trainable parameter** | 13.6 M | Still off by 3 orders. |
| **10 samples per LoRA rank per target module** | 10 × 16 × 7 × 16 = 17,920 | Same order as the real data. Weak but not silly. |
| **Supervised-token count** (~20 tokens/param, Chinchilla-flavoured) | 20 × 13.6M = 273 M tokens ÷ ~250 tok/sample ≈ 1.1 M samples | Still 100× above Sleep-CoT. |
| **Samples per decision cell** (§1c) | 1,000–1,500 per (question × class) | **Matches the observed data.** This is the one to use. |

**Why samples-per-parameter fails here, mechanically:**

1. **The parameters are not free.** LoRA's ΔW = BA with B initialised to zero is a rank-16 perturbation of an already-good 1.236 B-parameter function. The *hypothesis class* is not 13.6 M-dimensional in any statistically meaningful sense — it is a low-rank neighbourhood of a strong prior. Sample-complexity bounds scale with effective, not nominal, dimension.
2. **82.6% of the trainable parameters are in that low-rank neighbourhood.** Only the 2.11 M encoder params are genuinely from scratch — and OpenTSLM warms *those* with 118,400 unlabelled-ish synthetic samples (§2d), then freezes the task budget for the rest.
3. **Weight decay 0.01 + LR 1e-4 for LoRA (`_get_optimizer`, `curriculum_learning.py:239-249`, which reuses the *projector* LR for LoRA, not the encoder LR) + a few epochs + early stopping** together constitute strong implicit regularisation. The model never reaches the capacity the parameter count implies.

**Where the heuristics start mattering again:** the from-scratch encoder. 2.11 M params of Conv+Transformer with **no** pretraining and **no** inductive bias for your 6–14-channel structure. At 10 samples/param that would want 21 M samples; at 1 sample/param, 2.1 M. Neither is available to you. **That is the argument for not training the encoder from scratch.**

**Defensible minimum, stated honestly:** taking OpenTSLM's own Sleep-CoT as the existence proof — 7,434 samples, 5 classes, 1 series, ~1,487 samples/class, 13.6 M trainable params — a floor of **~1,000–1,500 labelled samples per decision cell** is defensible, with the caveats that (a) Sleep-CoT's split leaks, so treat 1,500 as optimistic, and (b) Sleep-CoT had a 118,400-sample encoder warm-up you must either inherit or replace.

---

# 4. The effective-sample-size problem

Your geometry, restated: one itinerary, weekly anchors (stride s = 7 d), three context windows per record (W ∈ {7, 60, 180} d). Overlap fraction 1 − s/W = 0%, 88.3%, 96.1% — your numbers check out exactly.

## 4a. The standard treatment

The canonical framework is **López de Prado, *Advances in Financial Machine Learning* (2018)**, Ch. 4 (Sample Weights) and Ch. 7 (Cross-Validation in Finance). Three concepts, all directly applicable:

- **Concurrency** c_t: how many samples' windows span timestamp t. For stride s and window W, c_t ≈ W/s.
- **Average uniqueness** ū_i = mean over the window of 1/c_t. For your geometry ū ≈ s/W.
- **n_eff = Σ ū_i ≈ N · s/W** — *the number of non-overlapping windows you could have drawn instead*. Equivalently, the well-known folk rule: *effective sample size is roughly row count divided by window length (in strides)*.

Applied to your channels:

| Context | W (d) | s (d) | Overlap | ū = s/W | n_eff / N |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| short | 7 | 7 | 0% | 1.000 | **1.00** |
| medium | 60 | 7 | 88.3% | 0.117 | **0.117** |
| long | 180 | 7 | 96.1% | 0.039 | **0.039** |

The classical statistical analogue is **Bartlett / Kish**: n_eff = N / (1 + 2Σ_{k≥1} ρ_k), or the finite-sample form n_eff = N / (1 + 2Σ_k ((N−k)/N)·ρ_k). For a window-averaged quantity with stride s and window W, the induced ρ_k ≈ 1 − ks/W for k &lt; W/s, which recovers n_eff ≈ N·s/W up to a factor of 2. Same answer, different route.

**But do not apply this naively to your whole record**, and here is why. There are two distinct effective-N questions, and conflating them is the classic error:

1. **n_eff for the metric** (the standard error on your test macro-F1). Governed by the autocorrelation of the *per-sample loss*. If test records overlap, your confidence intervals are too narrow by roughly √(W/s) — up to **5×** too narrow on the 180-day channel. Bootstrap **by itinerary and by time block**, never by row.
2. **n_eff for learning** (how much the model can actually absorb). Governed by the number of *distinct (input, label) pairs*. Here the 7-day channel is fully fresh each week and the label is fresh each week, so consecutive records are *not* duplicates. n_eff for learning is bounded **below** by the 7-day channel's N and **above** by N — it is not 0.039·N.

**Rule to hold onto: the redundancy applies per channel, not per record.** The union of information across your three windows renews weekly. What actually collapses n_eff is not the window overlap — it is **the clustering by itinerary**.

## 4b. Clustering by itinerary is the binding term

Records from one itinerary share route, operator, station set, rolling stock, timetable structure. That is a random effect, and the right formalism is the **design effect** (Kish, 1965):

&gt; **DEFF = 1 + (M − 1)·ICC** ,  **n_eff = N·M / DEFF**

with N = itineraries, M = anchors per itinerary, ICC = intra-class correlation of the residual (how much of the outcome variance is explained by *which itinerary* rather than *which week*). For transport delay data ICC = 0.1–0.3 is a reasonable planning range; measure it before you defend a number (fit a null mixed model, ICC = σ²_itinerary / (σ²_itinerary + σ²_residual)).

The crucial limit: as M → ∞, **n_eff → N / ICC**. **The number of itineraries caps your effective sample size no matter how many anchors you add.**

## 4c. 600 × 20 vs 200 × 60 — both 12,000 records

| ICC | 1000 × 12 | **600 × 20** | 400 × 30 | **200 × 60** | Winner margin |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.05 | 7,742 | 6,154 | 4,898 | 3,038 | 600×20 is **2.03×** 200×60 |
| 0.10 | 5,714 | 4,138 | 3,077 | 1,739 | **2.38×** |
| 0.20 | 3,750 | 2,500 | 1,765 | 938 | **2.67×** |
| 0.30 | 2,791 | 1,791 | 1,237 | 642 | **2.79×** |
| — | cap N/ICC (ICC=0.2) = 5,000 | 3,000 | 2,000 | **1,000** | |

**Answer: 600 × 20, decisively, and it gets more decisive as ICC rises.** At ICC = 0.2 you get 2.7× the effective sample size for the same annotation cost, the same GPU cost, and the same wall clock.

**The reasoning, not just the answer:**

- Each *new itinerary* contributes a genuinely new draw from the population of routes — new station topology, new operator, new timetable, new failure modes. Its marginal information content is ~1 full sample.
- Each *additional anchor on an existing itinerary* contributes only the week-to-week residual variation, worth ~(1 − ICC) of a sample at the margin, and decreasing.
- The N/ICC cap is the killer: 200 itineraries at ICC = 0.2 can never exceed n_eff ≈ 1,000, even at 1,000 anchors each. 600 itineraries cap at 3,000. **You cannot buy your way past the itinerary count with anchors.**
- It also matters for the *free-text rationale head*. Rationales conditioned on the same route repeat almost verbatim. 60 anchors on one itinerary teach the model one route's phrasing 60 times. That is how you get a model that produces fluent, confident, wrong rationales for unseen routes.

**But 200 × 60 has one real advantage, and you should keep it:** 60 weekly anchors = 13.8 months = full seasonal coverage; 20 anchors = 4.6 months = one season. A model trained on 4.6 months of anchors will not have seen the winter timetable, the summer engineering works, or the holiday peaks.

**Resolve this by staggering, not by choosing.** Give each of the 600 itineraries a 20-anchor window, but *randomise the start date* of that window across itineraries so their union spans 12+ months. You then get:
- per-itinerary anchor span = 20 weeks → DEFF stays at 1 + 19·ICC
- **union anchor span = 12+ months → full seasonal coverage**
- archive span cost = context_span + union_anchor_span + horizon (see §6)

This is strictly dominant over both of the options you posed. It is the single most useful design change in this report.

## 4d. Does the overfitting show up honestly in a time-disjoint test split?

**Partly. Two of the three leaks survive a time-disjoint split, and one of them is silent.**

Let the split boundary be time T: train anchors ≤ T, test anchors &gt; T.

**Leak 1 — boundary window bleed (visible if you look, silent if you don't).** A test anchor at T + 1 week carries a 180-day context that overlaps **96.1%** with the last training anchor's context. Its 60-day context overlaps 88.3%. Those first few test records are near-duplicates of training records. With weekly anchors it takes **26 weeks** before the 180-day contexts are fully disjoint. If your test window is 8 weeks long, **100% of it is contaminated** on the long channel.
→ **Fix (López de Prado, "purging and embargo"):** insert a gap of at least `context_span` (180 d) between the last train anchor and the first test anchor. Purge, don't just split.

**Leak 2 — label lookahead (visible, easily forgotten).** A training anchor at T has a label determined by outcomes up to T + horizon. If test begins before T + horizon, training labels already encode the test period.
→ **Fix:** embargo `max(context_span, horizon)`. For 180 d context and a short horizon, 180 d dominates.

**Leak 3 — itinerary identity memorisation. This is the silent one.** If the same itineraries appear in train and test, a time-disjoint split **will not** reveal the overfitting. The 180-day context is effectively a *route fingerprint*: the model learns "this delay signature = the 07:42 Zürich–Bern" and recalls the per-route base rate, which is stable across the time boundary. Your time-disjoint test score stays high. Your production score, on a new itinerary, collapses.

This is exactly the failure Dehghani et al. quantified (94.6% subject-dependent → 56.3% subject-independent for a DT with overlapping windows), and it is exactly the difference between OpenTSLM's Sleep-CoT (random split, 69.88 F1) and ECG-QA-CoT (patient-disjoint, 32.84 F1).

**What to actually do — report three numbers, always:**

| Split | Detects |
|---|---|
| **A. Time-disjoint, shared itineraries**, embargo ≥ 180 d | temporal generalisation only |
| **B. Itinerary-disjoint, shared time** | route generalisation only |
| **C. Both disjoint** (future weeks × held-out itineraries) | **this is your production number** |

**Gap(A − C) is your leakage estimate.** Publish it. A jury that sees you report C and quantify A − C will believe C. A jury that sees only A will (correctly) not believe it.

Additional practical notes:
- Do **not** use i.i.d. k-fold. Use `GroupKFold` on itinerary_id combined with blocked time folds.
- **Weight the loss by average uniqueness** (López de Prado Ch. 4): weight each sample by 1/(anchors for that itinerary in the batch's window), or simply cap anchors per itinerary. Otherwise itineraries with more anchors dominate the gradient in exact proportion to how redundant they are.
- **Bootstrap CIs by itinerary block, not by row.** Row-bootstrap on 96%-overlapping windows understates the CI by up to ~5×.

---

# 5. Comparable work — 8 concrete datapoints

| # | System | Setting | Training-set size actually used | Result |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **MiniGPT-4** ([arXiv:2304.10592](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2304.10592)) | Frozen LLM + trainable projector, stage-2 alignment. **The closest architectural analogue to OpenTSLM-SP.** | **3,500 curated pairs, 400 steps, batch 12** | ~7 min on 1× A100; this stage is what makes the model usable |
| 2 | **LLaVA** ([arXiv:2304.08485](https://arxiv.org/html/2304.08485v2)) | Same two-stage recipe; projector align then instruction tune | **158K instruction samples** (58K conversation, 23K description, 77K reasoning) | Full multimodal instruction following |
| 3 | **Chronos** fine-tune ([arXiv:2403.07815](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2409.15367) methodology) | Chronos-T5-Small on held-out (zero-shot) datasets | **1,000 fine-tuning steps**, LR 1e-3 → 0 linear; datasets filtered to ≥50 series | Fine-tuning "unlocks further improvements," but "the zero-shot backbone alone offers a highly competitive initialization" |
| 4 | **Time-LLM** ([ICLR 2024, arXiv:2310.01728](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2310.01728)) | Frozen LLM reprogramming, ETTh1 | Full: **8,545 windows** → MSE 0.408. **10% ≈ 854** → 0.556. **5% ≈ 427** → 0.627 | **A real learning curve: 20× less data costs 54% MSE.** 10× less costs 36%. |
| 5 | **TEST** ([ICLR 2024, arXiv:2308.08241](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2308.08241)) | TS-for-LLM soft prompts, frozen LLM, 8 backbones | **All 128 UCR + all 30 UEA datasets** — UCR median train split is a few hundred samples (range 16–8,926) | +18% acc (univariate) / +25% (multivariate) for GPT2-774M |
| 6 | **MOIRAI / LOTSA + GIFT-Eval** ([arXiv:2410.10393](https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10393v2)) | *Pretraining* corpus, for scale contrast | LOTSA **231 B observations**; GIFT-Eval-Pretrain 3.25 M series / 230 B obs; **GIFT-Eval train-test split only 144 K series** | The pretraining corpus is 6 orders larger than the fine-tune corpus |
| 7 | **TimeGPT** ([Nixtla docs](https://www.nixtla.io/docs/forecasting/fine-tuning/steps)) | Commercial TSFM fine-tune API | Default and documented examples: **`finetune_steps=10`**; "larger value works better for large datasets" | The vendor's own default is 10 steps |
| 8 | **RIDE** rail-delay benchmark ([arXiv:2606.05070](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.05070)) | **Directly your domain.** Belgian rail, 1,355 operational points, 94.5 M events / 3.6 M journeys / 2023–2025 | They **subsample to 50,000 training snapshots** (standard tier) / **15,000** (lite tier). Train 2023–24, test 2025 — time-disjoint. Context = 15 previous events (~40 min); horizon = next 15 events | GNN 73.62 s MAE, Transformer 74.54, LSTM 74.62, **XGBoost 76.58**, MLP 77.20 |

**Two things to take from this table:**

- **The defensible range for a frozen-LLM + trainable-adapter fine-tune is 3,500 – 160,000 samples, with 8,000 – 20,000 the modal choice.** MiniGPT-4 at 3,500 is the floor of the credible range; LLaVA at 158K and OpenTSLM's HAR-CoT at 68,542 the upper. Sleep-CoT at 7,434 sits comfortably inside and is the paper's best result.
- **Datapoint 8 is your best jury exhibit.** A team with **94.5 million** rail events chose to train on **50,000** snapshots, and their best deep model beat XGBoost by **3.9%** (73.62 vs 76.58 s MAE). Every deep architecture landed within 5% of every other. That is what a saturated data regime looks like: at 50K samples the bottleneck is already problem structure, not data volume. **You will not need more than they used, and they used 0.05% of what they had.**

---

# 6. Bottom line

## 6a. Set the constraints first

**VRAM (not binding).** Soft-prompt token cost = Σ ceil(L/4) per sample (`patch_size=4`, `model_config.py:12`; `TransformerCNNEncoder.forward` yields L/4 patch tokens; each becomes one LLM token via `MLPProjector`):

| Config | Soft tokens | Measured VRAM (SP/Llama-1B, batch 4, paper Table 10) |
|---|---|---|
| HAR-CoT (3 × 128) | 96 | 9.6 GB |
| Sleep-CoT (1 × 1500) | 375 | 15.9 GB |
| ECG-QA (12 × 1000) | 3,000 | 64.9 GB |
| **You, min (6 × 168)** | **252** | ~13 GB (interpolated) |
| **You, max (14 × 240)** | **840** | **~25 GB** (linear interp between Sleep and ECG: 15.9 + (840−375)/(3000−375) × 49.0) |

**~25 GB at batch 4 on a 48 GB L40S.** You can run **batch 8** comfortably, batch 12 with gradient checkpointing (`--gradient_checkpointing`, README L237). Also note `max_patches=1024` caps series length at 4,096 points — your 240 steps uses 60 of 1,024 positions. **GPU is not your constraint. Do not let anyone tell you it is.**

**Optimizer steps (binding).** Consumable records = (steps × batch) ÷ epochs.

| Budget | batch | epochs | Consumable records |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1,000 steps | 4 | 4 | 1,000 |
| 3,000 steps | 4 | 4 | 3,000 |
| 4,000 steps | 8 | 4 | 8,000 |
| 6,750 steps | 8 | 3 | 18,000 |

**Do not build a dataset larger than you can consume.** At 8,000 records and a 4,000-step budget you get 4 clean epochs. At 40,000 records you get 0.8 epochs and a from-scratch encoder that never converges. **A larger corpus is actively harmful under a fixed step budget.**

**Archive span** = context_span (180 d = 5.9 mo) + **union** anchor span + horizon.

## 6b. The three tiers

Assume weekly anchors, 180-day longest context, ~2-week horizon, staggered anchor windows (§4c), ICC = 0.20 for planning.

### (a) Minimum viable — 250 itineraries × 12 anchors

```
records   = 250 × 12                        = 3,000
DEFF      = 1 + (12−1)(0.20)                = 3.20
n_eff     = 3,000 / 3.20                    ≈ 938
per-itinerary anchor span = 12 wk           = 2.8 months
union anchor span (staggered)               = 6 months
archive   = 5.9 (ctx) + 6.0 (anchors) + 0.5 = 12.4 months
steps     = 3,000 / 4 × 4 epochs            = 3,000 steps
VRAM      ≈ 25 GB (batch 4)                 ✓ fits
wall clock ≈ 3,000 × ~1.9 s                 ≈ 1.6 h + eval
```
**Binding constraint: n_eff (statistical).** 938 effective samples split across 4 heads ≈ 235 per head. Below OpenTSLM's floor (Sleep-CoT had ~1,487 per class on one head). **Expect the regression head and the two classifiers to work at ~2–3× chance and the rationale head to be fluent but generic.** Only run this tier if you *must* ship, and report split C (§4d) with wide, itinerary-block-bootstrapped CIs.

### (b) Recommended — 500 itineraries × 16 anchors

```
records   = 500 × 16                        = 8,000
DEFF      = 1 + 15(0.20)                    = 4.00
n_eff     = 8,000 / 4.00                    = 2,000
per-itinerary anchor span = 16 wk           = 3.7 months
union anchor span (staggered)               = 12 months  ← full seasonal cycle
archive   = 5.9 + 12.0 + 0.5                = 18.4 months
steps     = 8,000 / 8 × 4 epochs            = 4,000 steps
VRAM      ≈ 40 GB (batch 8)                 ✓ fits in 48 GB
wall clock ≈ 4,000 × ~3.2 s                 ≈ 3.6 h + eval
n_eff cap = 500 / 0.20                      = 2,500  (you are at 80% of it — good)
```
**Binding constraint: archive span (calendar).** You need **18.4 months of history**, driven almost entirely by the 12-month union anchor span you need for seasonality. Not by the sample count, not by the GPU, not by the steps.

This tier lands squarely in the credible band from §5: 8,000 records is between MiniGPT-4's 3,500 and OpenTSLM Sleep-CoT's 7,434 on the low side and LLaVA's 158K on the high side, and *below* RIDE's 50,000 rail snapshots. n_eff = 2,000 ≈ 500/decision-cell across 4 heads, within a factor of ~3 of Sleep-CoT's 1,487.

**This is the tier to defend.**

### (c) Comfortable — 900 itineraries × 20 anchors

```
records   = 900 × 20                        = 18,000
DEFF      = 1 + 19(0.20)                    = 4.80
n_eff     = 18,000 / 4.80                   = 3,750
per-itinerary anchor span = 20 wk           = 4.6 months
union anchor span (staggered)               = 14 months
archive   = 5.9 + 14.0 + 0.5                = 20.4 months
steps     = 18,000 / 8 × 3 epochs           = 6,750 steps
VRAM      ≈ 40 GB (batch 8)                 ✓
wall clock ≈ 6,750 × ~3.2 s                 ≈ 6.0 h + eval
n_eff cap = 900 / 0.20                      = 4,500
```
**Binding constraint: wall-clock + marginal return.** 6,750 steps is at the top of your stated budget, and by n_eff = 3,750 you are past the knee. The reference points: Time-LLM's curve is already shallow above ~1,000 windows (0.556 → 0.408 MSE over a 10× increase); RIDE's deep models spread only 3.9% between best and worst at 50,000 snapshots. **You will not measure the difference between tier (b) and tier (c) above the noise floor of your own test set** unless your test set is much bigger than tier (b)'s.

## 6c. Summary table

| | Itineraries N | Anchors M | Records | n_eff (ICC 0.2) | Union anchor span | **Archive span** | Steps | **Binds on** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **(a) Minimum viable** | 250 | 12 | 3,000 | 938 | 6 mo | **12.4 mo** | 3,000 @ b4 | **n_eff** |
| **(b) Recommended** | **500** | **16** | **8,000** | **2,000** | **12 mo** | **18.4 mo** | 4,000 @ b8 | **archive span** |
| **(c) Comfortable** | 900 | 20 | 18,000 | 3,750 | 14 mo | **20.4 mo** | 6,750 @ b8 | **wall-clock / flat return** |

## 6d. Four decisions worth more than any of the three tiers

Ordered by expected F1 per unit of effort:

1. **Do not train the encoder from scratch.** Paper Table 3: Chronos-2 as a frozen encoder gives **+15.24 F1 on Sleep-CoT** (49.33 → 64.57) and **+7.30 on ECG-QA** (34.62 → 41.92) at *identical* dataset size. That is worth more than tripling your corpus, and it deletes the 118,400-sample stage-1 warm-up (§2d) from your budget entirely. If you must keep `TransformerCNNEncoder`, at minimum initialise from `OpenTSLM/llama-3.2-1b-m4-sp` on HF Hub (README L168–181).

2. **Spend on itineraries, never on anchors.** n_eff is capped at N/ICC. 600 × 20 beats 200 × 60 by **2.67×** at ICC = 0.2 for identical cost (§4c). Then stagger the anchor windows so the union covers 12 months.

3. **Concentrate supervision, don't spread it.** ECG-QA-CoT's failure is 159,306 rows over 3,138 questions = **50.8 samples/question**. Your four heads (1 regression, 2 classifiers, 1 rationale) are only viable if each classifier has few classes. **Budget ≥ 500–1,000 samples per class per classifier.** If a classifier has 12 classes you need 6,000–12,000 records for that head alone — collapse it to 4–5 classes instead of collecting 3× the data.

4. **Report split C, and report the A − C gap.** Time-disjoint alone hides route memorisation (§4d, Leak 3). Embargo ≥ 180 days at the boundary. Weight the loss by average uniqueness or cap anchors per itinerary. Bootstrap by itinerary block. **A jury that sees you volunteer the leakage number will trust the honest number; a jury that sees only the flattering number will trust neither.** OpenTSLM's own Sleep-CoT vs ECG-QA-CoT gap (69.88 vs 32.84) is the cautionary tale, and it is sitting in `sleepedf_cot_loader.py:85`.

<result># AMENDED BRIEF — §4 and §6 re-scoped to the stop-event unit

§1, §2, §3, §5 stand exactly as delivered. Three of their conclusions get *sharper* under the new unit, and I flag those first; then §4 and §6 are rewritten in full.

## What the unchanged sections now mean

**§1 — the ECG-QA lesson is now directly, uncomfortably applicable.** ECG-QA-CoT failed with 159,306 samples because they were spread over 3,138 questions (**50.8 samples/question**), while Sleep-CoT succeeded with 7,434 over 5 classes (**1,487/class**). Your new unit has the same shape risk: a Munich slice has ~40,000 distinct (service, station) pairs in the weekday timetable. If you sample one anchor each, you get **1 sample per pair** — the ECG-QA failure mode, exactly. The fix is not more data; it is **fewer pairs, more anchors each, but not too many** (§4c). This is now the central tension and it did not exist in the old brief.

**§3 — the parameter count is unchanged (13,647,616 trainable) and the samples-per-parameter heuristic is now *even more* clearly the wrong tool.** You could trivially supply 13.6 M samples from a Munich year (§6a). You still should not. The constraint was never parameter count.

**§5 — datapoint 8 (RIDE) is now your single best exhibit, and I checked its definition.** A RIDE "snapshot" is a *network state*, not a stop event: *"it contains information on active trains (including schedule, past delays, …), which is then used as model inputs for predicting future delays,"* with **"an average of 210 active trains per snapshot"** and 15 forward events each. So one snapshot ≈ 3,150 (train, event) predictions. They drew **50,000 snapshots from 94.5 M events** — roughly one snapshot per 21 minutes across 2023–2024 — and their best deep model (GNN, 73.62 s MAE) beat plain XGBoost (76.58 s) by **3.9%**, with MLP/LSTM/Transformer/GNN all inside a 4.9% band. The paper does **not** report the delay distribution, does **not** discuss redundancy between consecutive stops of a run, and does **not** report any snapshot-count ablation. So: **a team with 94.5 M rail events used 0.05% of them, took maximal within-run redundancy, and got a 3.9% edge over a gradient-boosted tree.** That is what saturation plus uncontrolled redundancy looks like, and it is the strongest single argument that your binding constraint is n_eff and not supply.

---

# §4 (rewritten). Effective sample size under three-way clustering

## 4a. Supply: the constraint has genuinely flipped

At 14.27 M national stop events/month:

| Munich share | Stop events/month | Stop events/year |
|---|---|---|
| 5% | 0.71 M | 8.6 M |
| 8% | 1.14 M | 13.7 M |
| 12% | 1.71 M | 20.5 M |

Against a consumable training corpus of 6,000–21,000 records (§6a), you will use **0.05–0.25% of one Munich-year**. Supply is not a constraint and will not become one. Every remaining question is a *sampling design* question. Design your subsample as a survey statistician would, not as a data collector would.

## 4b. The formalism: cross-classified, not nested

Your three correlation sources are **crossed, not nested** — a run crosses many stations, a station-hour crosses many runs, a (service, station) pair crosses many dates. Nested `DEFF = 1 + (m−1)ICC` does not apply directly. Two tools:

**For inference (standard errors), use Cameron–Gelbach–Miller multiway clustering** ([JBES 29(2):238–249, 2011](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1198/jbes.2010.07136)), which is inclusion–exclusion over the clustering dimensions:

&gt; V = V_run + V_stationhour + V_pair − V_{run∩sh} − V_{run∩pair} − V_{sh∩pair} + V_{run∩sh∩pair}

**For design (how many samples to draw), use the additive cross-classified design effect.** For a variance-components model y = μ + a_run + a_sh + a_pair + ε with ρ_X = σ²_X/σ²_total:

&gt; **DEFF ≈ 1 + (m_run − 1)·ρ_run + (m_sh − 1)·ρ_sh + (m_pair − 1)·ρ_pair**
&gt; **n_eff = N / DEFF**

where m_X is the *average number of your sampled records sharing a level of X*. This is exact for the mean of a balanced crossed design and a good planning approximation otherwise. **The m's are yours to choose. That is the whole lever.**

Planning values for ρ — **measure these on one month of Munich data before defending any number** (fit a null cross-classified mixed model, `delay ~ 1 + (1|run) + (1|station_hour) + (1|service_station)`, and read off variance ratios):

| Dimension | ρ (planning) | Why |
|---|---|---|
| ρ_run (delay propagation along a run) | **0.70** | Knock-on delay is near-deterministic: delay at stop k+1 ≈ delay at stop k + increment − buffer. Literature on high-speed delay propagation finds cumulative delay follows the primary delay with high R². Range 0.6–0.85. |
| ρ_sh (station-hour congestion) | **0.30** | Shared infrastructure state, weather, disruption. Real but weaker. Range 0.2–0.4. |
| ρ_pair (chronic lateness of this train here) | **0.20** | The systematic "the 07:42 is always 4 min down at Pasing" component. Range 0.15–0.30. |

## 4c. The decisive result: the stop-sampling rule dominates everything

Same N = 15,000 records, varying only the sampling rule:

| Sampling rule | m_run | m_sh | M | **DEFF** | **n_eff / N** | n_eff at N=15,000 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **All ~20 stops of each run**, M=5 | 20 | 1 | 5 | **15.10** | **6.6%** | 993 |
| 6 stops per run, M=5 | 6 | 1 | 5 | 5.30 | 18.9% | 2,830 |
| 3 stops per run, M=5 | 3 | 1 | 5 | 3.20 | 31.2% | 4,688 |
| **One stop per run**, M=20 | 1 | 1 | 20 | 4.80 | 20.8% | 3,125 |
| **One stop per run**, M=10 | 1 | 1 | 10 | 2.80 | 35.7% | 5,357 |
| **One stop per run**, M=6 | 1 | 1 | 6 | 2.00 | 50.0% | 7,500 |
| **One stop per run, M=5** | 1 | 1 | 5 | **1.80** | **55.6%** | **8,333** |
| One stop per run, M=3 | 1 | 1 | 3 | 1.40 | 71.4% | 10,714 |

**Take one stop per run. It is worth 8.4× in effective sample size (6.6% → 55.6%) and costs nothing.**

Why so large: (m_run − 1)·0.70 with m_run = 20 contributes **13.3** of the 15.10 total DEFF — 88% of all your redundancy sits in one term, and that term is entirely a consequence of a sampling choice. Twelve consecutive stops of one run *is* nearly one observation: it is one train, one crew, one primary delay event, watched twelve times. The model will learn "delay persists along a run," which is true, trivially predictable from the previous stop's delay, and not what you need a TSLM for.

**Station-hour clustering is a non-issue for you, and here is the calculation that shows it.** Munich has on the order of 150 stations × 365 days × 18 operating hours = **985,500 station-hours per year**. At N = 15,000 records spread over 12 months that is **0.015 samples per station-hour** — essentially every sampled record is alone in its station-hour, so m_sh ≈ 1.0 and the middle DEFF term vanishes. This holds even at N = 50,000 (0.051/station-hour). Sparse sampling buys you out of the congestion-correlation problem for free.

Two caveats on that:
- It fails the moment you take all stops of a run through a hub — those *are* co-located in station-hours. Another reason for the one-stop rule.
- It does **not** hold at inference. When you compose an itinerary from 3–5 stop predictions, those predictions share station-hours and runs, so **their errors are positively correlated and the itinerary-level uncertainty is much wider than the naive √(Σσ²)**. Propagate uncertainty with the ρ's above, or you will systematically understate journey-level risk. This is a product correctness issue, not just a statistics one.

## 4d. How many anchors per pair? The marginal-anchor curve

Marginal n_eff added by the M-th anchor on a pair, at ρ_pair = 0.20:

| M | DEFF | n_eff per pair | Marginal gain of this anchor |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1.00 | 1.00 | — |
| 2 | 1.20 | 1.67 | +0.67 |
| 3 | 1.40 | 2.14 | +0.48 |
| 4 | 1.60 | 2.50 | +0.36 |
| **5** | **1.80** | **2.78** | **+0.28** |
| 6 | 2.00 | 3.00 | +0.22 |
| 10 | 2.80 | 3.57 | +0.11 |
| 20 | 4.80 | 4.17 | +0.04 |
| ∞ | — | **5.00 = 1/ρ** | 0 |

**The M-th anchor is worth 1/(1+(M−1)ρ)² of a fresh pair.** The asymptote is 1/ρ_pair = 5 effective samples per pair no matter how many anchors you add — the same N/ICC cap as before, now expressed per pair.

**M = 5 captures 56% of the theoretical maximum (2.78 of 5.00) at 25% of the anchors needed for M = 20.** M = 5–6 is the knee. Anything above M = 10 is close to wasted GPU.

**Corollary, and it's the counterintuitive one: prefer more pairs over more anchors, exactly as before — but the reason has changed.** Previously it was itinerary-level route effects. Now it is that a new (service, station) pair is a new *timetable slot*: a different scheduled dwell, different platform, different connecting traffic, different buffer allowance. That is genuinely new structure. A sixth Tuesday for the same slot is the same structure re-sampled.

## 4e. Splits, purging, and what the new scope makes easier

**What got easier.** Under the old unit you needed itinerary-disjointness in the test split, because you would deploy on unseen itineraries. Under the new scope — complete coverage of one region, itineraries composed at inference from stops that *are* in the training distribution — **station- and service-disjointness are no longer the production condition.** You will always predict for Munich stations you have seen. The model memorising "the 07:42 runs 4 min down at Pasing" is now a *feature*, not leakage. This is a real simplification the scope change buys you, and you should claim it.

**What replaces it.** Three things:

1. **Run-disjointness — automatic under the one-stop-per-run rule.** With one stop per run, no run can straddle the split. Under any other rule you must group-split on `(service, date)`.
2. **Time-disjoint with a purge.** Boundary at date D: train anchors ≤ D, test anchors ≥ D + max(context_span, horizon). With a 180-day longest context that is a **180-day embargo**. Without it, the first ~26 weekly test anchors have 96%-overlapping long-context windows with the last training anchors. Split on *date* boundaries, never on row index.
3. **The timetable change is your real distribution shift.** Germany's annual *Fahrplanwechsel* is mid-December. Every (service, station) pair's identity, dwell and buffer can change. **Your test split must cross a timetable change**, or you are measuring in-timetable interpolation and calling it generalisation. Plan to retrain at each change; report the pre-change and post-change test scores separately. This is the single most defensible operational statement in the amended brief.

**Report four numbers.** Supply is free, so there is no excuse not to:

| Split | What it isolates | Role |
|---|---|---|
| **A.** Time-disjoint, within same timetable period | in-timetable temporal generalisation | optimistic bound |
| **B.** Time-disjoint, **crossing a timetable change** | the real deployment condition | **headline number** |
| **C.** Service-disjoint (held-out lines) | did it learn physics or slot identity? | diagnostic |
| **D.** Station-disjoint (held-out stations) | portability to Nuremberg later | roadmap number, not a launch gate |

**A − B is your timetable-drift estimate. A − C is your memorisation estimate.** Publish both. Bootstrap all CIs by **run and by date block**, never by row: row-bootstrap on within-run-correlated samples understates the CI by ≈ √DEFF, which under the all-stops rule is **3.9×**.

## 4f. Regression vs calibrated classification — the sample-size pictures differ, materially

They differ in three ways, all against the classifier.

**(1) The binary's effective N is the event count, not the record count.** Every record informs the regression. Only the ~12% positives inform the positive side of the binary. At N = 15,000 you have 15,000 for the regression and **~1,800 events**. The binary is effectively running on an 8× smaller dataset. After the DEFF discount (n_eff = 8,333) the *effective event count* is **~1,000**.

**(2) Calibration is a curve, not a scalar, and the old rules of thumb are known to be too loose.** [Riley et al., *Statistics in Medicine* 2021, doi:10.1002/sim.9025](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/sim.9025) is explicit: the familiar "100 events and 100 non-events" rule was *originally motivated by calibration curves*, and **"sample sizes of 100 (or even 200) events and non-events can give imprecise estimates, especially for calibration."** Precision depends on the linear-predictor distribution, not just the event count. The development-side counterpart ([Riley et al., BMJ 2020 / Stat Med 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30357870/)) targets a **global shrinkage factor ≥ 0.9** (≤10% overfitting), apparent-vs-adjusted R² gap ≤ 0.05, and precise estimation of overall risk. Point accuracy (MAE, AUC) converges at O(1/√n) globally; calibration must be right *locally in every bin of predicted risk*, so it converges at the rate of the sparsest bin — and the sparsest bin is the high-risk tail you care about most.

**(3) Two architecture-specific hazards that no sample size fixes.**

- **The rationale head will drown the targets.** OpenTSLM's loss (Eq. 8) is plain token-level autoregressive NLL over the whole output. A 150–300 token rationale contributes 150–300 terms; your scalar and your yes/no contribute ~1–4. Unweighted, the model optimises fluency and lets the probability drift. The paper names this failure in Limitations: *"framing tasks as natural language generation does not ensure that the model prioritizes the correct label after presenting its rationale, underscoring the need for loss functions that explicitly enforce correct answers."* **Up-weight the answer tokens by ~50–100× or train the answer heads on a separate loss term.**
- **You cannot currently read a probability out of the model.** `OpenTSLMSP.compute_loss` returns `outputs.loss` and discards the logits (`src/opentslm/model/llm/OpenTSLMSP.py:363`), and both val and test loaders hardcode `batch_size=1` and go through `generate` (`curriculum_learning.py:1031, 1039`). To get a calibrated P(&gt;5 min) you need a **teacher-forced scoring path** that returns the softmax over {" yes", " no"} at a fixed answer position. That is a ~20-line patch and it is a prerequisite, not an optimisation — it also makes large-scale evaluation cheap (§6b).
- **Then recalibrate post hoc on a held-out split.** Temperature or Platt (1–2 params) needs ~200–500 events; isotonic needs ~1,000+. Budget it separately from train and test.

**Net:** for equal reliability, **the calibrated binary needs roughly 1/π ≈ 8× the records the regression needs.** Plan the corpus around the binary and the regression comes free.

## 4g. Imbalance: do not correct it

Base rates, for grounding. Deutsche Bahn 2024: **long-distance 37.5% of stops reached ≥6 min late** (62.5% punctual — worst in two decades); **regional 90.3% punctual**, i.e. **9.7% late**. A Munich slice dominated by S-Bahn/RB/RE gives **π(&gt;5 min) ≈ 8–15%**; use **12%** for planning and measure it. That is **mild imbalance, nowhere near rare-event territory** (which begins below ~1–2%).

**Recommendation: keep the natural base rate. No undersampling, no oversampling, no SMOTE.**

The evidence is direct and it is about exactly your metric. [van den Goorbergh, van Smeden, Timmerman &amp; Van Calster, *JAMIA* 29(9):1525–1534, 2022](https://academic.oup.com/jamia/article/29/9/1525/6605096): random undersampling, random oversampling and SMOTE **"yielded poorly calibrated models: the probability to belong to the minority class was strongly overestimated,"** produced **no** AUC improvement, and any sensitivity/specificity benefit was **"obtained by shifting the probability threshold instead."** Their conclusion: *"imbalance correction led to models with strong miscalibration without better ability to distinguish… outcome imbalance is not a problem in itself, and imbalance correction may even worsen model performance."* Since calibration is your stated goal, this settles it.

**Do this instead:**

- **Stratify on predictive covariates, not on the outcome.** You are already discarding 99.9% of supply — spend that freedom on *variance reduction*, which is free and preserves the base rate. Stratify the subsample on: hour-of-day (peak/off-peak/late), day-of-week (Mon–Thu / Fri / Sat / Sun), product (S-Bahn / RB / RE / IC-ICE), station tier (hub / medium / halt), and month. Proportional or Neyman allocation. This is classic stratified sampling: it lowers the variance of every estimate without touching π.
- **If you must enrich the tail, do it as outcome-dependent sampling with a recorded weight.** Take all events plus a fraction *f* of non-events, then either correct the intercept by **log(f)** (King &amp; Zeng prior correction) or fit with inverse-probability weights 1/*f* on non-events. Calibration then survives. **Never enrich without recording *f*.** Cap enrichment at ~2–3× the base rate; beyond that you have too few effective non-events near the decision boundary to recalibrate against.
- **Threshold, don't resample.** If operations want high recall on "&gt;5 min", ship a calibrated probability and move the threshold. That is the van den Goorbergh finding restated as an engineering instruction.
- **Handle the regression's skew separately.** Delay is heavily right-skewed (mass at 0–2 min, tail past 60). An LM emitting digits as text is poor at heavy tails. Predict **log1p(delay)** or a small set of quantiles/bins, and report MAE *and* tail metrics (P90 absolute error, coverage of an 80% interval) — plain MAE is dominated by the near-zero mass and will look excellent while the model is useless on the cases anyone cares about.

---

# §6 (rewritten). Bottom line

## 6a. The constraints, re-derived

**Supply — not binding.** 0.7–1.7 M Munich stop events/month. You need ≤21,000. Ratio ≥ 34:1 per month.

**VRAM — not binding.** Soft-prompt cost is unchanged: 6×168/4 = **252** to 14×240/4 = **840** tokens (`PATCH_SIZE=4`, `model_config.py:12`). Interpolating paper Table 10 (Sleep 375 tok → 15.9 GB; ECG 3000 tok → 64.9 GB, SP/Llama-1B, batch 4): **~25 GB at batch 4**, ~48 GB at batch 8 without checkpointing (too tight), **~28 GB at batch 8 with `--gradient_checkpointing`** (README L237). Use batch 8 + checkpointing. Measure it first with the repo's own `evaluation/memory/get_memory_use.py`.

**Optimizer steps — binding for the training set.** Consumable records = steps × batch ÷ epochs. At batch 8: 3,000 steps → 6,000 records @ 4 epochs; 5,625 → 15,000 @ 3; 7,875 → 21,000 @ 3. **Above ~21,000 records you exceed "a few thousand steps."** Building a bigger training corpus than this is not neutral — it is actively harmful, because you will run &lt;1 epoch and the from-scratch encoder will never converge.

**Statistics — binding for the test/calibration set**, which is a *different* budget (§6b).

## 6b. The move the new scope unlocks: decouple train size from test size

Under the old brief you had one scarce corpus split 80/10/10. That is now wrong. **Training-set size is GPU-bound; test- and calibration-set size is statistics-bound; supply is free.** Size them independently.

- **Train: 6,000–21,000** records — as much as your step budget can chew, no more.
- **Calibration: 5,000** records (~600 events at π=0.12) — enough for isotonic, comfortably enough for Platt/temperature.
- **Test: 20,000–50,000** records (2,400–6,000 events) — because Riley 2021 says 100–200 events is imprecise *for calibration*, and you want tight calibration curves in the high-risk bins.

The cost objection dissolves once you add the teacher-forced scoring path (§4f): forward-pass-only scoring at batch 8 evaluates **20,000 records in ~6 minutes, 50,000 in ~16 minutes**. Only the free-text rationale needs `generate`, and you can judge rationale quality on a 500-record subsample. Under the shipped code path (`generate`, batch_size=1) the same evaluation would take ~17 hours — which is precisely why you should not use it.

## 6c. The three tiers

All tiers assume the **stop-sampling rule** below, staggered anchor dates, ρ_run 0.70 / ρ_sh 0.30 / ρ_pair 0.20, π(&gt;5 min) = 0.12, 180-day longest context, batch 8 + gradient checkpointing.

### (a) Minimum viable — 1,200 pairs × 5 anchors, 6-month anchor span

```
records   = 1,200 × 5                              = 6,000
DEFF      = 1 + (1−1)0.70 + (1−1)0.30 + (5−1)0.20  = 1.80
n_eff     = 6,000 / 1.80                           = 3,333
events    = 6,000 × 0.12 = 720   →  n_eff-events   ≈ 400
steps     = 6,000 / 8 × 4 epochs                   = 3,000
union anchor span                                  = 6 months
archive   = 5.9 (ctx) + 6.0 (anchors) + 0.5 (hor)  = 12.4 months
supply used = 6,000 / ~8.6 M per year              = 0.07%
```
**Binds on: effective events for the calibrated binary.** ~400 effective events is below the level at which Riley 2021 considers calibration precisely estimable, and the 6-month anchor span means **no timetable change is crossed**, so split B (§4e) does not exist. Ship this only as a feasibility demo; report the regression head as primary and the binary as uncalibrated, with wide run-block-bootstrapped CIs.

### (b) Recommended — 3,000 pairs × 5 anchors, 12-month anchor span

```
records   = 3,000 × 5                              = 15,000
DEFF                                               = 1.80
n_eff                                              = 8,333
events    = 15,000 × 0.12 = 1,800 → n_eff-events   ≈ 1,000
steps     = 15,000 / 8 × 3 epochs                  = 5,625
union anchor span                                  = 12 months  ← crosses one Fahrplanwechsel
archive   = 5.9 + 12.0 + 0.5                       = 18.4 months
+ calibration set 5,000 (600 events), test set 20,000 (2,400 events) — inference-only
supply used = 40,000 total / ~8.6 M per year       = 0.47%
```
**Binds on: archive span (calendar).** You need **18.4 months of history**, driven by the 12-month anchor span you need to cross a timetable change and cover the seasonal cycle. Not by sample count, not by GPU, not by steps.

n_eff = 8,333 with ~1,000 effective events puts the binary above the Riley threshold and the regression comfortably clear. 15,000 records sits between OpenTSLM's Sleep-CoT (7,434, the paper's best result) and HAR-CoT (68,542), below RIDE's 50,000 snapshots, and above MiniGPT-4's 3,500 — squarely inside the defensible band from §5. **This is the tier to defend.**

### (c) Comfortable — 3,500 pairs × 6 anchors, 14-month anchor span

```
records   = 3,500 × 6                              = 21,000
DEFF      = 1 + 0 + 0 + (6−1)0.20                  = 2.00
n_eff                                              = 10,500
events    = 2,520  →  n_eff-events                 ≈ 1,260
steps     = 21,000 / 8 × 3 epochs                  = 7,875
union anchor span                                  = 14 months
archive   = 5.9 + 14.0 + 0.5                       = 20.4 months
+ calibration 5,000, test 50,000 (6,000 events)
```
**Binds on: optimizer steps / wall-clock.** 7,875 steps is the top of "a few thousand," ~7 h on an L40S. And you are past the knee: n_eff rises only 26% over tier (b) while DEFF worsens (1.80 → 2.00) because the 6th anchor is worth 0.22 of a fresh pair. **Spend the extra budget on the 50,000-record test set, not on the training set** — the test set is where the marginal return actually is at this point.

## 6d. Summary

| | Pairs N | Anchors M | Records | DEFF | **n_eff** | Eff. events | Anchor span K | **Archive span** | Steps | **Binds on** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **(a) Minimum** | 1,200 | 5 | 6,000 | 1.80 | 3,333 | ~400 | 6 mo | **12.4 mo** | 3,000 | **effective events / calibration** |
| **(b) Recommended** | **3,000** | **5** | **15,000** | **1.80** | **8,333** | **~1,000** | **12 mo** | **18.4 mo** | 5,625 | **archive span (calendar)** |
| **(c) Comfortable** | 3,500 | 6 | 21,000 | 2.00 | 10,500 | ~1,260 | 14 mo | 20.4 mo | 7,875 | **steps / wall-clock** |

Plus, at every tier: **calibration set 5,000, test set 20,000–50,000**, drawn by the same rule from disjoint dates, evaluated forward-pass-only.

## 6e. The stop-sampling rule (this is the deliverable)

1. **One stop per run. Non-negotiable.** Worth **8.4×** in n_eff (DEFF 15.10 → 1.80). If a run is selected twice, drop one. If you need in-run context, put the previous stops in the *input series*, never as separate training records.
2. **Pick the stop uniformly at random from that run's stop list**, not always the hub. This spreads station coverage, keeps m_sh ≈ 1, and stops the model from learning only München-Hbf dynamics.
3. **M = 5 anchors per (service, station) pair**, spread ≥ 30 days apart so the union spans 12+ months (73-day gaps at M=5 over a year). Never M &gt; 10 — the 11th anchor is worth 0.09 of a fresh pair.
4. **Stagger anchor dates across pairs** so the union covers the full year and crosses a Fahrplanwechsel, while per-pair span stays short and DEFF stays at 1.80.
5. **Stratify on covariates** (hour × day-type × product × station tier × month), proportional allocation. **Never stratify on the outcome** without recording the sampling fraction *f* and correcting the intercept by log(*f*).
6. **Keep π ≈ 0.12 natural.** No SMOTE, no RUS, no ROS. Threshold at inference.
7. **Split by date with a 180-day purge**, and make sure one split boundary crosses the December timetable change.
8. **Bootstrap by run and date block.** Row-bootstrap understates CIs by √DEFF.

## 6f. The four decisions still worth more than any tier

Unchanged in rank, updated in wording:

1. **Do not train the encoder from scratch.** Paper Table 3: Chronos-2 frozen gives **+15.24 F1 on Sleep-CoT** (49.33 → 64.57) and **+7.30 on ECG-QA** at identical dataset size, and deletes the 118,400-sample stage-1/2 warm-up (`curriculum_learning.py:1547-1563` shows LoRA is off for stages 1–2 precisely to warm the encoder). Otherwise start from `OpenTSLM/llama-3.2-1b-m4-sp` (README L168–181).
2. **One stop per run.** 8.4× in n_eff, zero cost. The largest single lever in this brief.
3. **Fix the loss and the read-out before scaling data.** Up-weight answer tokens ~50–100× against the rationale, and add the teacher-forced logit path (`OpenTSLMSP.compute_loss` currently discards logits, line 363). Without both, no amount of data produces a calibrated probability — the paper's own Limitations section predicts exactly this failure.
4. **Size the test set for statistics, the training set for GPU.** 15,000 train / 5,000 calibration / 20,000+ test. Report split B (crossing a timetable change) as the headline, and publish A − B and A − C as your leakage and drift estimates.

## 6g. The one-sentence defence

&gt; A team with 94.5 million rail events trained on 50,000 snapshots and beat XGBoost by 3.9%; OpenTSLM's best published result came from 7,434 samples while its 159,306-sample dataset scored half as well because those samples were spread over 3,138 questions. We are asking for **15,000 stop events — 0.2% of one Munich-year — drawn one per run so that 8,333 of them are statistically independent, spanning 12 months of anchors across a timetable change, backed by an 18.4-month archive.** The constraint is not how much data exists; it is how much of it is not a copy of data we already have.

**New sources for this amendment:** [Cameron, Gelbach &amp; Miller, *JBES* 29(2):238–249, 2011](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1198/jbes.2010.07136) · [Riley et al., *Stat Med* 2021, doi:10.1002/sim.9025](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/sim.9025) · [Riley et al., minimum sample size for development, *Stat Med* 2019 Part II](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30357870/) · [van den Goorbergh et al., *JAMIA* 29(9):1525–1534, 2022](https://academic.oup.com/jamia/article/29/9/1525/6605096) · [RIDE benchmark](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.05070) (snapshot definition, 210 trains/snapshot, 50,000/15,000 tiers) · [DB 2024 punctuality: long-distance 62.5%, regional 90.3%](https://zbir.deutschebahn.com/2024/en/interim-group-management-report-unaudited/product-quality-and-digitalization/punctuality/) · López de Prado 2018 Ch. 4 &amp; 7 · Kish 1965.

**Two numbers I could not source and you must measure:** (i) Munich's exact share of the 14.27 M national monthly stop events — you said you are measuring it, and every "supply used" percentage above scales inversely with it; (ii) the three ρ values, from a null cross-classified mixed model on one month. **ρ_run is the one that matters** — if it comes back below ~0.4 the one-stop-per-run rule can be relaxed to 2–3 stops, and if it comes back above 0.8 the rule becomes even more decisive than I have stated.</result>

<note>A task-notification fires each time this agent stops with no live background children of its own. The user can send it another message and resume it, so the same task-id may notify more than once.</note>
<result>Done. All 23 tests pass, and the module integrates cleanly with the `entities.Entity` / `anchors.Anchor` types that landed while I was working.

## What I built

**`/Users/gaurav/Downloads/zurich_lates/temporal_ai_error404.ai/app/agents/src/features/db_route/resample.py`** (650 lines)

- **One SQL template** (`_BUCKET_SQL`), one `time_bucket(INTERVAL &lt;Layer.bucket&gt;, hour, TIMESTAMP &lt;origin&gt;)`, zero per-profile branches. A test greps the source to keep it that way.
- **`SIGNAL_SPECS`**: every signal is data (`grain`, `numerator`, `denominator`, `impute`), not code. An import-time check fails loudly if `profiles.SIGNALS | CAUSE_SIGNALS` ever names something with no formula.
- **`FactCache`** → panels. Cache dir defaults to `~/.cache/db_route/`; `relations=` injects table names so tests run offline.
- **`Window`** carries `values` (float32, `layer.n_steps`), `present`, per-channel `imputed_fraction`, `bucket_starts`, plus `worst_/overall_imputed_fraction`, `degenerate_channels`, `reject_reason()`.
- `is_degenerate`, and non-finite values raise with the channel/layer/entity/anchor named.

**Phase anchoring — the load-bearing insight:** the SQL origin is a *fixed* midnight, not the anchor's. Every anchor's `t0` is a local midnight and every bucket (1h/6h/1d) divides 24 h, so all anchors share one grid. That is what makes "aggregate once, slice with numpy" legal rather than an approximation.

**Efficiency** — measured on a synthetic fixture at the real cache's dimensions (400 stations × 7,400 hourly buckets, 1,000 service groups, 3,683 pairs, 17M fact rows): 4.0 s to build all seven panels, **0.29 ms per XM window** (15 series) → ~4 s for 15,000 records, 0.62 GB resident. Rows stay sparse + `searchsorted`; densifying pairs would allocate 27M cells for 3.7M real ones.

**`.../db_route/tests/test_resample.py`** (23 tests, synthetic in-memory DuckDB, no network). Run with `python tests/test_resample.py`; pytest collects it too (pytest is not in the venv).

## Invariants tested

Count-weighted re-bucketing (both 6 h and 1 d); exact `n_steps` and endpoint (last bucket is `t0 - bucket`, travel day excluded); phase alignment per layer, across five different anchors; gap interpolation vs. throughput zero-fill; zero-denominator ≠ zero share; minutes-weighted vs. event-weighted shares; grains stay distinct; degeneracy; non-finite raises; all four profiles from one fixture with XM's shared layers **bit-identical** to S/M/L; panel count is 7, not per-profile.

**Mutation-verified.** `sum→avg` is a *no-op* (avg/avg ≡ sum/sum) — worth knowing, that mutation does not test what it looks like it tests. The faithful mutation (aggregate per-hour ratios) returns exactly **11.0** against the true **2.178**, and kills both unbiasedness tests.

## Contract problems found

1. **`MAX_IMPUTED_FRACTION = 0.10` is structurally unreachable at the week layer.** A `(service, station)` pair sees trains ~05:00–23:00, so `service_at_station_delay` is legitimately absent for **25%** of 168 hourly buckets on a perfectly healthy record. A single max across channels rejects essentially every record. `test_pair_grain_is_structurally_sparse_at_the_week_layer` pins the number. I did **not** change the constant — the per-channel dict is intact so the caller can adopt a per-grain budget or a service-hours denominator.
2. **Grain of the cause shares was unspecified.** `&lt;family&gt;_share` and `attributed_share` are computed at **station** grain (rationale in `_cause_share`: at pair grain the attributed-minutes denominator is zero more often than not once coarsened, given causes.py's measured 30.1% attribution). One-line change if that is wrong.
3. **Stricter than `nullif`:** I require `denominator &gt; 0`, not `!= 0`. `sum_delay_attributed` can go negative (attributed stops that ran early), which would yield a "share" outside [0,1] — worse than absent.
4. **Timezone frame of `hour` is undocumented.** `facts.py` writes `date_trunc('hour', e.time)` and piebro doesn't say whether `time` is naive Berlin or UTC. Naive-local → the grid is right and DST costs 2 buckets/year (absorbed). UTC → the learned phase is UTC hour-of-day, off by the offset. Flagged in the module docstring; someone should read the column and delete the hedge.
5. **`anchors.Anchor` spells the date field `anchor`, not `travel_date`** — `anchor_midnight` now takes both rather than forcing the two modules to agree on a name.
6. **`build.py` already duplicates my guards** at lines ~294–303 (constant-series and non-finite, as raises). Worth deduping onto `resample.is_degenerate`. I added `Window.overall_imputed_fraction` to match build.py's `imputed_fraction` annotation ("share of context buckets"), which is not the same thing as the max.

I did not touch `sources.py`, `causes.py`, `profiles.py`, `facts.py`, or the other agent's `entities.py`/`anchors.py`/`labels.py`.</result>
